- **Named in this batch for the first time since the sixtieth day of the flood, and the finding of the fourteenth day of the month after this one is his.** He came out of the chapel floor on the twenty-fifth day of this flood, has been fed every day by people who did not ask him whether he wanted the food, **has said three words in seventy-nine days**, and those three words were on a stair on the thirty-fifth day of the new month, which was the sixtieth day of the flood, and they were *are they coming*. **Nobody has asked him one question since.**
- **He picked up ten days of work nobody sent him to — four people trying to write down a room at the far end of a seam — and said in his own words that picking work up is not a post and is not repaid and does not put anybody on a roll, and a girl of seventeen entered that on the fourth day of the month after this one and again on the fourteenth and entered that she had entered it twice.**
- **Said the finding in eleven sentences: the four lines are not the room and they are not nothing either, they are the shape of the room after eleven people have been through it, and I am the only one of the four of us who has been one of the people it is about.** Nobody argued and it is entered that it is true.
- **Asked for it to be entered that the not-asking is a decision and not a fault.** He is the living page of a rule this city has not written down.

## Sabra Vint — about sixty-one, of the upper road, cannot read

- **Handed nine years of her own paper to a girl of seventeen on the second mile of a nine-mile road in the rain, in about four seconds, and kept nothing back, and said why: *I would like somebody to have the date and not me.*** The copying is finished, the slates are the work, **and the meaning of nineteen shapes is still unwritten and nobody has written anything on the wall.**
- **Said that she is the only one doing this for nine years, that she is sixty-one, and that she would like to stop being the person a thing depends on, and that there is no other way to stop it than to hand it over on a road, and that this is a ridiculous way to do it and she has thought about it for nine years.**
- **Was asked, for the first time in nine years, what she thought one of the seven cuts meant, in plain words, on a road, and said she did not know**, and then said that this is the first time in nine years that the honest answer and the answer she was going to give anybody anyway are the same thing, and that she has written down what she thinks the shapes mean about four hundred times in her head and has never once written it, and that she is sixty-one and is not going to be ashamed of it in front of nine people, and asked for it entered that she was not ashamed. **It is entered and she is not thanked.**
- **Her twenty-one words from the sixty-fifth day of the new month and her nine years of paper are now in a girl's book and a docker's slate and neither of them is in a case.**

## Marda Rood — about sixty-one, of Four Elms

- **Agreed in about four seconds to a seventh thing going on the second page inside her stone — a count of seven cuts, in six lines, under the twelve names, with the word *nobody* on the line above and a line under saying it is a count and not a debt** — and asked for all three of those things, and the reason she gave is the same one she gave on the hundredth day of the flood: **there have been three pages in this stone in thirty days, and in about four years the person holding it is going to be a stranger, and a stranger cannot be angry at a page, and that is exactly why it works.**
- **Did not read the count before it went in and was not asked to, and the keeping household may take that page down on a date and is not asked why twice.**
- **Still will not have her name on anything.** Three pages in a stone and no name.

## Elsa Skell — 44, of Corrow

- **Came four miles to a room by the salt wharf and said, in the doorway before she had sat down, that she is owed a figure on the end of a cost column and a decision about ninety-one acres that belongs to her and not to anybody in that room, and that she is not here about either of them, and that she would like to be left alone.** Nobody asked her anything else for four hours and it is entered.
- **Disagreed with three about the dressed edge for the fifth time since the first of March, all five entries stand, the twenty-fifth reading was not taken**, and said the reason in a mason's words she will not improve on: **you do not write a number into a column when four people do not agree about where the edge is.** She has said that a dressed join does not narrow and it gets dressed in three days and has said she is not going to say it again in a fortnight for anybody.
- **Is still owed a figure and a decision, and no further questions about either until she asks, and it is on a page in a stone.**

## Peth Lomas — 50, a mason of Saltwake, not of the crew

- **Said on the thirteenth day of the month after this one that three of them were at a line and he was a sixteenth under, four times since the first of March, and that he has not got any better and is not going to pretend to have got any better, and that it is entered that the first time he was under and the fifth time he is under and that nothing in between moved him.** He is under **five times out of six** as of the twentieth day of the month after this one.
- **Answered the twenty-fifth reading on the twentieth day of the month after this one in nine words: *Twenty-four, and one ruled, and the dressed edge is dressed.*** He is one of the four and only four and no fifth reading is permitted.

## Jonah Pike — 42, of the White Ledger

- **Was in the room on the twelfth day of the month after this one and had come nine miles to watch a document be ended, and had not been asked anything, and said one sentence about the ground a room can hold, and is right.** He is not convicted of anything and is not going to be.
- **Has said nothing in this batch about the seat, and nobody has offered it a sixth time.**

## Mina Okoye — of the second list

- **Said nothing in this batch and her sentence was not used again.** It has been used twice in the book, both times by her, both times about somebody who is not her.

## Ivenn Marrow — 41, Crown Law scholar from Aurel, unaccused

- **Said on a wharf, in the rain, in front of nine people, that he has not explained either name in a hundred and one days and is not going to, and asked for that to be entered a second time — the same reason as the first time: a man who says a name and then gets to explain himself afterwards has said the name for himself.**
- **Was asked the two words on a wharf by a person who had not been in the room on the first day of the month after this one, and a woman of about thirty-four refused to say them and was not asked again, and he did not say them either.** Nobody guessed either name aloud in this batch except a person who had been given it.
- **Gave the one true thing anybody had about sending a person: a man can ride to Long Water in a day and Verrow in two and the Reach in four, and then he is standing in a village of nine households asking a boat-builder's wife whether she will carry a sealed book nine hundred miles for him, and in nineteen years he has watched that happen in nine districts and it is the single fastest way in the world to have your book opened by a man in a coat.** He offered nothing on the wharf and said nothing wrong.
- **Asked for a source three times and was not asked a fourth time.** He has still not mentioned the letter to Hattie Park. **The count is sixty-one days at Chapter 0101, anchored to the fortieth day of this flood and to nothing else.** **His room is in about fourteen weeks and is not due in this batch and not in the next ten days.**

## The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf

- **Refused to say the two names on a wharf in the rain and gave the reason in about four seconds, and said go and ask him and that either way it will be in the book in his own words because he will ask for it to be.**
- **Said the reason nobody read the eighth column out: you do not say a thing like that where two hundred and forty people can hear you, and you do not say it in a language either, because a thing said in a language is a thing that has left your mouth.**
- **Said the three sentences that hold a cooperative together and the second half of the twenty-five-word finding about institutions and persons, and refused to give the two hundred words tidily.**
- **Said the hardest thing any of the nine people would do in the four days after the twentieth day of the month after this one: we do nothing about it, because there is a date on a page in a house four miles up a road and every single thing any of us wants to do about it makes it a case, and a case goes to Aurel, and an office that has a household's date in it has a household's door in it.** About four people wanted to write a notice about it and did not.
- **She is on nothing and is named on nothing and has now said, on the page, that a number that nobody has to agree with is the only thing in four hundred miles this city has not tried to turn into a person, and that she is never going to be gladder about a number.**

## The girl of seventeen

- **Entered the count of five rooms, the twelve lines, the two struck lines, the four lines about a room, the seven cuts, the hearing and the number of households, and the copy's heading that says *it is a copy and the stone does not travel*, and asked for all of it with the date on it.**
- **Was asked for the sixth time in a hundred and seven days whether she wanted to know which book a field goes in, and said no, and gave the reason: if the man tells her, a clerk of the Registry at Aurel has decided which of his two books a thing goes in and the whole of what this city has said about the difference of ten being a mystery is a rule with a man at the top of it; and if he does not, she is the girl who asked a sixth time. It is entered that she said no and why, and the count of six is on the page.**
- **Said on a road that the order of eight means something different from the order of eleven and that nobody has ever asked, and that she asked it on the nineteenth day of the month after this one and nobody answered her.**
- **Took nine years of a woman's paper on a nine-mile road and has it, and is on no roll, and is still the only person in this city who has ever refused anything the wall has said.**

## Tam Crane — 9

- **Said he could take a thing up the nine miles again before anybody asked him and said he wanted it written that he said it before anybody asked, which is a thing a man in a good coat does, and it was written.** He carried a cost column eleven miles on the first of March, which was the sixtieth day of the new month and the eighty-fifth day of the flood, in the wrong weather, and it is in a book and does not say his name.
- **Counted the eleven households on the road and got nine and counted a shutter, and walked back two hundred yards with a woman of twenty-four to check his count, and his count was better than the number on the page, and nobody changed the eleven.** He counted them again on the way down and got nine again and told everybody about it twice.
- **Asked who is going to be told, on the sixth mile, in six words, and wrote it on the back of a slate, and it is in the book in his own hand.** Asked whether there was a bed at the top of the road, and there is.
- **His answer to the question asked of him on the twenty-fifth day of the new month is still unwritten fifty-one days later.**

## Offstage but due

- **Nora Vale, 25, of Camden.** A letter is in a tin and it is not sent and there is no road. **The other world is not named once in Chapters 101–110.** *Open, and carried.*
- **Hattie Park, of Dunedin.** **Sixty-one days at Chapter 0101, counted from the fortieth day of this flood and from nothing else, and Marrow has still not mentioned it and the count is not mentioned again in this batch.** *Open.*
- **The nine households of Nine Elms** — refused three times, water not received for thirty-four to forty days across this batch counted from the sixty-ninth day of the flood, quarter gone, the fourth line in force, the whole charge on a man of thirty-two, **and the document that would have paid for it is void, and nobody in this city has said what happens at a fourth refusal.**
- **A woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms.** Not asked, not approached, not named in any room where it could reach her, and nobody stands outside the fourth house. She appears once in this batch, as a line on a page in a stone. **Open and deliberately unasked.**
- **The six of the first group and the eleven of the second group** are still named, still with no land rights, no class, no standing and no workway, except one, and the one is not whole, has refused the asking once, and is owed a document that does not contain her.
- **A girl of sixteen** is one of the two people on no roll in this city, and has still not been asked about it. **The two people on no roll may never be witnesses, and a girl of seventeen who is on no roll is not one of them.**
- **Elsa Skell's figure and Elsa Skell's ninety-one acres**, **Ilda Marsh's cart and a fourth refusal**, **the second list which did not go at all**, and **the four who read the join-line, who are still four.**

# Volume 03, Batch 0002 — the second ten days of Volume 03, days 111–120

## Adrian Vale — 32, protagonist

- **Still Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing and not in a ceremony. Unbound. Barred from every roster. No mark of his own, no workway, no title, no land right, no class privilege, no seat, no office, and there is no office in Saltwake for what he now is.** **He performed no working in any of these ten days and no threshold was opened and the crossing is not written in them and the other world is not named once in Chapters 111–120.**
- **What it cost, again, in these ten days:** the knowing and not a road. **He has no return mark, the route home goes through a room with no door.**
- **Mark:** the black line in the heel of his right palm is **one hundred and ten days old at Chapter 0111 and one hundred and nineteen at the end of Chapter 0120 and has not moved one sixteenth of an inch at any point in this batch.** No chapter says by how much and no chapter says what it is. **The line's age and the length of his stay in this world are two different numbers and were not run into one another anywhere in the batch: a hundred and ten days in this world at Chapter 0111 and a hundred and nineteen at the end of Chapter 0120, and the line is as old as the second figure and not the first.**
- **THE BATCH'S COST TO HIM, AND IT IS THE WORST ONE YET: HE READ A DOCUMENT OUT LOUD IN A ROOM AND IT MADE HIM A COLUMN.** On the twenty-ninth day of the month after this one, which was the hundred and nineteenth day of the flood, he read a boundary page of the village of Nine Elms in about nine minutes in the local language in front of about nine people, because he is the only person in that room who can tell a form from a sentence, **and he did not claim to be the only person in four hundred miles — he said so two lines below a man of forty-one in a very good coat who has read every book that office keeps and who had said out loud that he could not read the form and would not guess, and the chapter's argument is that the rarity is the danger and not a comfort.** A woman of seventy of Nine Elms carried the page in and gave it to a girl of seventeen and not to anybody else, and a woman of twenty-four had asked him thirteen days earlier not to put eight names on a page in this city. **A translation is a description. A description is a removal with a column for where the person is. The column is him.** He said it first and nobody improved on it and it is entered as what a person said and not as a finding. **He said in four minutes that he is not going to be able to stop, and that a rule about a document is a rule about a document, and that reading is the whole of what he can do in this world and is not a neutral thing to be able to do.** **Nobody absolved him and he did not ask them to, and nobody looked for an instrument that would let him stop, and the search was not a rescue and is not one.**
- **On the thirtieth day of the month after this one he entered the difference between the two settlements of the ninety-one-acre season**, and it is his one speech in that scene: the first was a person and could be asked whether she would have it, this one is a rule under a line and cannot be asked anything, and the woman it is on is standing in the room and has not been asked by anybody **including him**, and that is the whole of what is different. He also said that this afternoon was **the first time in this city that one of those rules had gone at a person instead of at a page**, and that a rule cannot be refused and a number cannot be asked whether it will have it and a person can.
- **What else this batch cost him:**
  - **He refused to read the writing round the edge of a wax in about four seconds on the twenty-sixth day**, and read the four lines under the seal and said why: a form is not a face, and a face is a thing a carrier reads and a carrier reads it out to the room. He said the same thing twice that afternoon and would not say it a third time.
  - **He said a hearing is a room in about four seconds** on the twenty-first day, and a woman of twenty-four wrote *he would rather be in the room* with no marks round it, and he said nothing else about it for two hours.
  - **He said the two people who can carry nothing are the reason and neither of them can carry anything**, and asked for it entered that a thing said by a man with no roster is not a standing and is not a claim.
  - **He said a man who is not paid and a man who will not come are two different men**, and that a thing nobody can be angry at is also a thing nobody can be stopped by.
  - **He said a page can be taken down on a date by the household that keeps it and a door cannot**, and then a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf had him exactly backwards in nine seconds and he said in four seconds that she was right and that the two of them had said the same sentence and neither had heard the other do it.
  - **He read the number off a door in chalk, four feet back, and said it out loud**, and it is the whole of what he did at Nine Elms.
  - **The whole of what he did to the case was not read the wax.**
  - **He did not answer a boy of nine's answer and said nothing about it for four minutes**, and a woman of twenty-four entered that and then did not enter it, and entered that she noticed the second one.
- **Voice:** no new slip in this batch. He has now entered the same refusal four times in ten days across two batches and the repetition is the method and not a tic.
- **Where he is:** Saltwake, **day 120, the Tuesday, the thirtieth day of the month after this one, at about the ninth hour of the evening, in a room over a market, having been entered as a column.**

## Sera Vail — 50, Lantern Concord notary, twenty-six years

- **Accepted the thirtieth thing the wall has said in about four seconds and was not refused, and entered that she did not think about it**, and a woman of twenty-four said that not thinking about it is the finding and not the excuse. It is the third time in a hundred and twenty days she has accepted a field in four seconds and the first two moved nothing and this one moved something on the following day.
- **Entered that four agreeing accounts of a crossing are not a record**, and that this is the first time in a hundred and sixteen days this city has had a thing it could not make a record about.
- **On the twenty-sixth day of the month after this one entered that she would like to be asked before the box is opened**, in a room, in daylight, with a date on it, by a person, and the reason is that she has been the notary of about nine hundred things and about nine hundred of them were somebody else's sentence. **She has been asked about it four times in four days and has answered none of the four, and the not-knowing is entered with the date on it, and that is the first time in twenty-six years she has entered that she does not know, and she said she is not going to be asked to be the notary of the Open Hand and that is the correct arrangement.**
- **Read the whole of a nineteen-line page headed *the box as received* aloud in about nineteen seconds, then read the first attempt again, and asked the question nobody in that room could answer — *What is this about?*, four words — and nobody answered it for about nineteen seconds and it is entered that nobody answered it.** She has now asked a four-word question nobody can answer twice, six days apart, and the second is a new sentence and not the first.
- **Said in about four seconds that the cost she accepted in four seconds the previous day had arrived in about four hours and that nobody had to do anything to make it arrive**, and that it was the third time in a hundred and twenty days that a thing she accepted without thinking had cost somebody something, and that nobody asked her about it twice.
- **Said the part nobody in the room was going to be able to carry on the twenty-ninth day**: the fifth clause of the notice of the twenty-eighth day of this flood, that a person who could not afford to answer an instrument has not answered it, so the instrument of the twenty-second day of the new month stands **answered forty-nine times and unanswered by ninety**, and that it is a document this city cannot answer and did not write. **She has now entered the noticing four times in a hundred and twenty days and said that it is not modesty.**
- **She still holds page forty-one. Nobody has mentioned it.**

## Ivenn Marrow — 41, Crown Law scholar from Aurel, unaccused

- **Said, on the twenty-ninth day of the month after this one, that he cannot read a boundary form, and gave the reason in about nine seconds: he has read every book that office keeps and has never held one of these, and this is a district form and not a book, and he was not going to guess at it in a room with about nine people in it.** He was not made useless and he was useful in exactly one scene and the scene is about the limit of what he knows.
- **He did not interrupt a man of thirty-two once in four minutes.**
- **He offered nothing in these ten days, was asked to explain neither name, and said nothing wrong in a hundred and twenty days. He is not convicted of anything and is not going to be.**
- **He has still not mentioned the letter to Hattie Park and the count is not spoken: it is seventy-one days at day 111 and eighty at day 120, anchored to the fortieth day of this flood and to nothing else, and it appears zero times in Chapters 111–120.**
- **His room is on the hundred and eighth day of the month after this one, which is the hundred and ninety-eighth day of the flood — about twelve weeks off at Chapter 0111 and about eleven weeks at Chapter 0120 — and it is stated once, in the last ledger paragraph of Chapter 0120, and is not due.**

## Renna Ord — 58, boat-coat dock worker, cannot read

- **Said thirteen words at about the seventh hour of the evening of the twenty-fourth day of the month after this one, at the top of two hundred steps: *A man who has stopped coming is not a cost that has stopped*.** A girl of seventeen counted them and the room did not check the count. Then she said the rest, which is not counted, that the foreman of the day crew has said four words under that gate every working day since the forty-sixth day of the new month, which is *mind the low stone*, that he said them this morning to nobody, and that the correct reading of a man who does not turn up is that he has not been paid, **and that the correct reading is wrong in the direction that is easiest for everybody in this city.**
- **Said the sentence again on the twenty-eighth day — a person who has never been asked is not a person who has refused — and about four people understood in about four seconds that the sentence had never been about the man of sixty-one it appeared to be about, and that nobody said so, and that the correct thing to do about that in this city was to let it stand.** It is the third time she has said it and she has said it about three different people and none of them was her.
- **Went down on her hands with her face four inches off the wax on the twenty-seventh day of the month after this one and said the wax is not the wax on ours and that the writing round the edge of it is a line she could not read because she cannot read.**
- **She did not ask the page anything and did not go near the box.** Her sixteen words from the thirteenth day of the month after this one were not said again and were not paraphrased.

## Nyla Ferrow — 70, keeps the lock chapel

- **Was asked, on the twenty-fifth day of the month after this one, a Thursday, to be the number — the one person who says the count out loud in a room in daylight with a date on it — and said no in about four seconds without stopping, and the reason took about nineteen seconds and it was about herself and not about the number.** She did not put down the cloth.
- **Her reason, in her own words:** she has kept a floor for fifty-one years and has done it by being a person nobody can find, and that is not modesty, it is the job; **and if the number is a person then the number can be asked whether the number is right, and in about eight weeks there will be somebody in a room nine hundred miles away asking her, and she has read a form four times in nine years and knows exactly what happens the first time somebody is asked a second time.**
- **She said the fifth thing on the nail in the arch is a question and not a duty, and that she has not been asked anything in a hundred and fifteen days and is seventy and intends to go on not being asked for as long as there is anything left to not be asked about.**
- **A man of thirty-two told her she was not being asked to be a seat, and she said that then she would take it and she is not going to, and that he had not asked her anything and she was not going to help him out.**
- **It is entered that about four people wanted to argue with her and did not, and that nobody asked her again that day, and that a docker of fifty-eight who cannot read said a person who has never been asked is not a person who has refused, and that a woman of twenty-four said the correct thing to do about a refusal given by a person who has never been asked anything in a hundred and fifteen days is to let it stand and go away.**
- **She read the join-line on the twenty-fourth day of the month after this one, said she was at the same line as both of the others, and said she had said the number out loud twice that day and had not been asked to.**
- **She washed a floor on a Thursday and a girl of seventeen wrote her reason out and did not put the number nine at the top of the page because it was not hers to put there.**

## The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf

- **Refused, in about four seconds, to put nine names in a carrier's book, and put the refusal on a board with no name on it and a date on it.** The reason is that a carrier's book is a roll and a roll goes in a case.
- **Refused a man of about thirty-nine his request to be added by name, in about four seconds, in nineteen words — a name on this board is a name in a book, a name in a book is a column — and then gave the rest: in this city a person is somebody you can find, in a book nine hundred miles away a person is a line, and the moment his name is on that board he is a line in a book and a line in a book does not know what it is for.** That is the **third** time in a hundred and twenty days she has been asked to put her name on anything and said no.
- **Said the finding of the twenty-second day of the month after this one in nine seconds: a page can be taken down and a door cannot, and those are the same sentence with the words moved about, and one of them is a protection and the other is a thing nobody can do anything with.**
- **Said, on the twenty-seventh day, that the description of the box is the only account and the account is about nine people, and that on the twenty-eighth day that in about eight weeks somebody is going to get up and give a speech about what this city has built and that every word of it is going to be about us, and asked for it entered that she is not going to use a boy of nine's sentence and is not going to let anybody else use it.**
- **Told a man of sixty-one on the twenty-seventh day that he had found a better version of a sentence she had said on a wharf on the eleventh day of the month after this one, and that she was not going to claim his, and that the two of them had said one sentence on two days and neither had heard the other do it.**
- **Refused three translations in nineteen days and said the answer is the same every time, and refused the fourth in about the same four seconds and said she is not going to say it a fifth time in a different room.** She is on nothing and is named on nothing and is not going to be gladder about a number.

## The girl of seventeen

- **Refused the man of thirty-nine's name in under a second when she had to, and said no to the fifth request to be added to a board, and said the reason: a name on that board is a name in a book and a book is a door.**
- **Wrote the number nine into a column in a book with no heading and no date on the twenty-first day of the month after this one, because a heading is a column and a column is a roll, and about four people wanted a date on it and nobody gave them one.** She entered it again properly with a date on the twenty-fifth day and with no person at the top of it.
- **Counted nineteen words, thirteen words, four words, five words, twenty-six words and twenty-eight words in this batch and the room did not check one of the six counts, and it is entered six times that the room did not check the count.** She read a boy of nine's slate out loud because he could not be sure of the local hand and asked for nothing and was given nothing.
- **Was handed a boundary page a hundred and forty years old in a fold of oilcloth by a woman of seventy and said she was the worst possible person to hand it to and that the woman of seventy did it on purpose**, and that the reason was that she is the only person in this city who copies things and who is on no roll, and that if it is ever in a book in this city the first copy of it will be in a book that cannot travel.
- **Said that she is not going to be the person who finds out what is in the box and that if she is asked to be she will say no in under a second.**
- **Is on no roll, and has not been asked one question about being on no roll in a hundred and twenty days.**

## Elsa Skell — 44, of Corrow

- **Was in a room over a market from the second hour of the afternoon to the seventh hour of the thirtieth day of the month after this one while the season of ninety-one acres out of a hundred and forty was settled in for the second time in thirty-five days, and was asked nothing, and said nothing, and did not turn round when a man of thirty-two explained in a room why a rule had just gone at a person instead of at a page.** It is entered that she heard it, and a girl of seventeen entered both of those things with the date on them, and that nobody improved on any of it.
- **The first settlement of that season was hers.** On the first of March, which was the eighty-fifth day of the flood, she stood at a gateway four miles down the Reach road at about the eighth hour of the morning, in daylight, in the local language, said ninety-one out of a hundred and forty, and **a number was said once and then not said again**, and she has said since that nobody in eighty-five days had asked her whether she wanted the field and that she was going to say a number anyway. **That entry is on the page and Chapter 0120 refers back to it, and the difference between the two settlements is the finding of that chapter: the first was a person and could be asked whether she would have it and the second is a rule and cannot be asked anything, and the rule under the eleventh line was ruled and empty on the first of March as well.**
- **Said five words — a figure and a decision — when asked what it cost, and the girl of seventeen counted them and the room did not check the count, and she said nothing else at all for the rest of the day.**
- **Disagreed with three about the dressed edge for the sixth time since the first of March on the twenty-fourth day of the month after this one, and the sixth was about the edge and not the number, and all six entries stand and the twenty-fifth reading was not taken.** Her reason is a mason's words she will not improve on: you do not write a number into a column when four people do not agree about where the edge is; a dressed join does not narrow and it gets dressed. She said she would not say it again that week for anybody.
- **Is still owed a figure and a decision, and no further questions about either until she asks, and it is on a page in a stone nine miles up a road.**

## Peth Lomas — 50, a mason of Saltwake, not of the crew

- **Did not answer the twenty-fifth reading on the twenty-fourth day of the month after this one, because the four did not agree, and said it is the hundred and fourteenth day and that he has been a sixteenth under five times out of six and that this morning was not the sixth.** The twenty-fifth reading was not taken and one ruled line is still ruled and still empty. The sixth disagreement of the six was **Elsa Skell's, about the edge**, and Peth Lomas is still under **five times out of six**.
- **Said in the yard that he is going to be a sixteenth under on the day somebody writes the twenty-fifth, and that he will not read it alone.**

## Garret Slee — 61, who has copied other men's sentences for nineteen years

- **Said the only thing anybody wrote down without arguing with it first, in a room on the twenty-seventh day of the month after this one: two people copying the same sentence makes the second one a photograph of the first one, and a photograph is true and is not a description, and nobody can be angry at it.**
- **Asked to see the writing round the edge of the wax, standing up, and said the reason before he was refused, and was refused in about four seconds by the man of thirty-two, and then gave the reason in his own words: he has copied about nine thousand sentences in nineteen years and every one of them he copied before he knew what it said, and not one of them in nineteen years has he been able to put down afterwards, and a mark somebody pressed is a sentence somebody said.**
- **Had a sentence ready nineteen yards in front of a boy of nine for two days and did not say it, and said in about nine seconds that the reason is that the sentence was about a road and a wall and a stone, and that it is a good sentence, and that it would have been about him.**
- **Was asked in a room for the first time, by a boy of nine, in front of about nine people, on a day he did not choose, and a docker of fifty-eight said a person who has never been asked is not a person who has refused, and nobody said that the sentence was not about him.**
- **Has still not been thanked for the nineteen stones and has still said not one word about the ground behind the ordinary wall.**

## Ilda Marsh — 70, of Nine Elms

- **Refused a fourth time on the twenty-second day of the month after this one, as a person and not as a village, and said the reason in about nineteen seconds: the four does what she says it does; three times the village said no and the no belonged to nine households; the fourth is hers; a village cannot be a person and a person can be a person, and a person can be held to it, and a person can be asked whether she will have it, and nobody in nine hundred miles has any instrument that can ask her that. She said it is not a victory and it is a cart at noon.**
- **Said that nobody was going to wash the date off, and why: a mark that comes off is a mark somebody can take back, a page can be taken down on a date by the household that owns it, and this door has no household that owns anything, so the mark stays and it is the only part of it that is out of her hands.**
- **Carried a boundary page a hundred and forty years old into a room over a market on the twenty-ninth day of the month after this one in a fold of oilcloth and gave it to a girl of seventeen and not to anybody else**, and said why in about nine seconds: she refused a fourth time seven days earlier, she had had a cart and a chest and a thing in the bottom of it that she had not shown anybody since she was a girl, and she would not show it to a room because a room would have written a thing about it.
- **Sat on a bench by the wall for four hours on the twenty-ninth day and said not one word, and nobody asked her why.** She is not a person anybody in that room was going to be able to help.
- **She is a different person from a woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms and the two were never put in one sentence.**

## Hesper Saye — 58, a bone-setter and herbalist

- **Was not at the gate on the twenty-fourth day of the month after this one. Her record is unchanged: four hundred men told a thing about a shoulder or a scalp, ninety of them wrong.** She is not one of the four who read the join-line and was not entered as one of them.

## Hettis Roke — 44, Hearthguard of the outer ward, nineteen years in

- **Was not asked anything in these ten days, in a room, on a different day, about his own eight, and nobody in about nine people put it to him.** He said **I do not know** on the nineteenth day of the month after this one and that is four words and it is on the page and nobody improved on it and it was not turned into a yes. **This is the only thing that is owed to him and it is still owed.**

## Mina Okoye — of the second list

- **Said nothing in this batch and her sentence was not used.** It has been used twice in the book, both times by her, both times about somebody who is not her.

## Peth Lammay — 24, a carrier of the second table, eleven years

- **Said the thing nobody in a room of about nine people had said in a hundred and thirteen days, in nine seconds: a case with a name in it can be carried** — and then the rest of it, that eight columns went down a river to an office with seven about a district and one a question, that the office can do nothing with the question and can do something with a name, that if this city had sent eight names it would have got an answer and the answer would have been wrong in about four seconds, and that it would have come back as a number about a district with eight people not in it and nobody in Aurel would have known anything had happened.
- **Was asked for the seventh time in a hundred and thirteen days which of the two books a thing goes in, and said the question had been asked seven times and had been unanswerable seven times, and that she had answered it wrongly twice and had not been told which twice were wrong.**

## Odile Ferrand — 38, of the second group, on no roll at all

- **Was one of the four on a quay at about the fifth hour of the afternoon of the twenty-sixth day of the month after this one and saw a case come up this river, and gave her account of it separately over about nine minutes, and it agrees with three others.** She has still not found out whether she was right about her daughter's name and its spelling, and that not-finding-out is still entered.

## Tam Crane — 9

- **Wrote his answer on the flat of a slate in about nineteen seconds on the twenty-eighth day of the month after this one, in the local language, and a girl of seventeen read it out because he could not be sure whether he had got the words in the right order, and he said she could have the slate and did not want it back.** It is **sixty-eight days late.**
- **His answer, whole and exactly as it stands on the slate:** *I asked my mother and she said she did not know, and I have thought about it every day since and I still do not know.* And *And I have got an answer to a different question. Every one of you was somewhere before you were here. That is not coming. That is only arriving.* The first block is twenty-six words and the second is twenty-eight and the room did not check either count. **It is not a lesson, it is not an instruction, nobody in the room summarised it for him, and it does not settle anything.**
- **Said no in about four seconds when he was asked whether he wanted to change any of it, and after that said nothing else, and said nothing about having been asked, and it is entered that nobody in that room said anything to him about having been asked and that a woman of twenty-four wanted to say the number out loud and did not.**
- **Said that his mother says a thing you have said out loud is not a thing you can put in a drawer, and that she has said it nine times and has never had to take one back, and a girl of seventeen said that is her mother's sentence and not his and did not write it down.**
- **He has four slates. One of the four is nineteen shapes and it belongs to a docker of fifty-eight who cannot read and is not repaid and is on no roll. The other three are his. The answer is on the flat of one of the three and the six words he asked on the sixth mile of the nine-mile road on the twentieth day of the month after this one are on the back of another of the three, and a girl of seventeen says out loud, when she is asked how she knows it is the same slate, that it is not.**
- **The six words have been answered.** A man of thirty-two answered them in about four seconds on the sixth mile in Chapter 0110, said *that is the hearing* and then four more words in the local language that nobody counted, and is entered that nobody counted them. **The answer is on the boy's slate and in no book, it has never been entered anywhere, and it has not been improved on or repeated since.** No later chapter may say they are unanswered.
- **He was the first of about nine people to go to sleep on the thirtieth day of the month after this one and left a slate face down on a bench with nothing on the other side of it, and nobody turned that one or the other one over.**

## A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall

- **Wrote a list of twenty-two lines with nine names in a left-hand column on the twenty-fifth day of the month after this one and crossed the left-hand column out in about four seconds, which is the first time in a hundred and twenty days he has crossed a line out of a list in his life, and said the reason was that he had put a name on it and he did not want a name of his on it.** He kept the rest of the page.
- **Was the person who read the bottom of a cost column out loud on the thirtieth day and then said the sentence that is now the entry**, and stopped for about nineteen seconds, and a woman of twenty-four said afterward that he had not been reading it out, he had been handing it over.
- **He has been in about nine rooms in fourteen days and about nine rooms in twenty days and has not slept in any of them.**

## A man of sixty-eight who has carried nine cases in his life

- **Was one of the four on the quay and read the face of the box out loud to a girl of seventeen on the twenty-sixth day of the month after this one**, and said that a face is a thing a carrier reads and a carrier reads it out to the room, and that the four lines under the seal are in a form and a form is not a face and he could not have read them in a hundred years of carrying them.
- **Did not read the writing round the edge of the seal and asked for that to be entered with the date on it, because he has carried nine of these and has never once read one of those.** He is a new working person in this batch and he is the standing evidence that a carrier reads a face and not a form.

## The woman of twenty-four

- **Entered the noticing four times in a hundred and twenty days and said that it is not modesty.** She is the batch's recorder and the batch's turn is not hers.
- **Worked out the clock in about four minutes on the twenty-first day of the month after this one** and said the whole of it out loud, and got it right.
- **Said on the twenty-eighth day that in about eight weeks she is going to be in a room and that if she is not careful about a boy of nine's sentence she is going to use it, and asked for the shame entered in advance with the date on it.**

## Offstage but due

- **Nora Vale, 25, of Camden.** A letter is in a tin and it is not sent and there is no road. **The other world is not named once in Chapters 111–120.** *Open, and carried.*
- **Hattie Park, of Dunedin.** **Seventy-one days at day 111 and eighty at day 120, counted from the fortieth day of this flood and from nothing else, and spoken zero times in this batch.** *Open.*
- **A woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms.** Not asked, not approached, not named in any room where it could reach her, and nobody stood outside the fourth house. **She appears in this batch twice, both times as an unnamed line in a ledger paragraph.** *Open and deliberately unasked.*
- **A man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close**, who is the only person in a room who says *I do not know* out loud without being asked, and who was named on the twenty-ninth day of the month after this one by Adrian Vale as the only person in a room who could have said it in the reading and could not, because he can read it. *Open.*
- **A man of about thirty-five whose right shoulder went on the thirty-first day of this flood, was reduced twice, cannot be set, did not go and was not made whole**, on a page in a stone as a debt with no line that will mend it. *Open, and unchanged.*
- **A woman of forty-one owed the work in her own words in the document that says the work is not written down anywhere.** *Open, and it is one of the twelve.*
- **The six of the first group and the eleven of the second group** are still named, still with no land rights, no class, no standing and no workway, except one, and the one is not whole, has refused the asking once, and is owed a document that does not contain her.
- **A girl of sixteen** is one of the two people on no roll in this city, and has still not been asked about it. **The two people on no roll may never be witnesses, and a girl of seventeen who is on no roll is not one of them.**
- **Jonah Pike, 42, of the White Ledger**, was not in these ten days and has said nothing since Chapter 0102. He is not convicted of anything and is not going to be, and the seat has not been offered a sixth time.
- **Wenna Cray's fifth statement of error, four sentences, all correct, stands unanswerable and unanswered and was not answered in this batch.**
- **Elsa Skell's figure and Elsa Skell's ninety-one acres, and the season of both**, **Ilda Marsh's cart and a fourth refusal**, **the second list which did not go at all**, and **the four who read the join-line, who are still four.**

# Volume 03, Batch 0003 — the third ten days of Volume 03, days 121–130

**Reviewed once, and nine faults fixed. `reviews/volume-03/batch-0003.md`. Nothing in this section was restarted and no entry was rewritten; the corrections are in place and the review file names them.**

## Adrian Vale — 32, protagonist

- **HIS NAME IS ON THE PAGE ONCE IN THIS BATCH AND IT IS IN CHAPTER 0129, IN TAMSIN QUILL'S MOUTH.** It does not appear in Chapters 0111–0128 or 0130, and it does not appear anywhere in Chapters 0111–0128 at all: twenty chapters. It appears in all fifty chapters of Volume 01, in forty-four of Volume 02's fifty and in nine of Volume 03's first ten. **The single naming is in the sentence where she says that keeping a document out of a book for seven years is the most consequential thing she has done in seven years, and that one of the about four people who worked out the cost of it is Adrian Vale, who told a room on the twenty-ninth day that a translation is a description, and he is not the one who should have had to say it.** The sentence is still not a speech about him. **A volume whose argument is that a name is a description has earned the right to withhold a name; it has not earned the right to withhold it for twenty chapters.** A later writer may not go back and name him in the finished chapters of this batch, and may not treat the one naming as a precedent for naming him in every chapter.

- **Still Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing and not in a ceremony. Unbound. Barred from every roster. No mark of his own, no workway, no title, no land right, no class privilege, no seat, no office, and there is no office in Saltwake for what he now is.** **He performed no working in any of these ten days and no threshold was opened and the crossing is not written in them and the other world is not named once in Chapters 121–130.**
- **Mark:** the black line in the heel of his right palm is **one hundred and twenty days old at Chapter 0121 and one hundred and twenty-nine at the end of Chapter 0130 and has not moved one sixteenth of an inch at any point in this batch.** No chapter says by how much and no chapter says what it is. **The line's age and the length of his stay in this world are two different numbers and were not run into one another anywhere in the batch.**
- **THE ONLY NEW INSTRUMENT ANYBODY HAD FOR HIM IN THIS BATCH, AND HE REFUSED IT HIMSELF.** On the thirty-third day of the month after this one a girl of seventeen put her own name at the top of a page and a man of about forty-four who keeps a stall asked him to read it out loud in the local language, and he said no in about four seconds, and the reason is on the page: **a page with a girl's name at the top of it does not get his voice on it, and on the twenty-ninth day of the month after this one a page turned out to be a description of a district and the column was the man who read it, and he is not going to stand in the next column of the next one.** He added that he has no work and has never had work in this world and that the whole of what he has ever been able to do for anybody in a hundred and twenty-three days is read a thing, and asked for the refusal to be written down with the date on it and with the fact that he said it in about four seconds and did not think about it. **That is a refusal of an act and not a rescue, and the girl read her own page out badly to about nine people and nobody corrected her.**
- **He entered, with the date on it, that he has not asked one person one question in a hundred and twenty-nine days** — that he has read things to rooms, said numbers out loud off walls, and been handed a page and read it, and that about four people in this city decided in the last ten days that reading is a form of asking, and it is not — and he asked for the reason to be entered too.
- **His two reasons for not being the one who asks, and both are on the page because he said them himself:** a person on no roster who goes to nine kitchens and asks nine households whether they will stand in a room nine hundred miles away is a person who is asking, and asking is what a chain is made of; and he is the only person in this city who can read two systems of writing, so any answer arrives in two systems at once and he is standing at the junction, and a man who says what nine people meant is a description of nine people. **He said the second one was new and that the two are one reason.**
- **What else this batch cost him:**
  - **He said a rule about a thing is not a rule about the world and a rule about a document is a rule about a document, and that a rule about a man is not a rule at all because a man is not one person**: a man this morning, a man who does not turn up is a different man, a man who has not been paid for thirty-nine days is a third one, and the rule that the first cannot be entered as owing anything is the rule that makes the second look like the third.
  - **He said a description of a thing is made by people who want the thing to be different from what it is, and that not one of the nine people in that room ever wanted the page to have not worked, and that this is why five rooms and then a sixth have got nowhere, and that it is not because the page is hard — it is because they are the wrong shape for the job and have been since the second day of this month.** He was asked who the right shape is and said **I do not know**, and entered that this is the first time in a hundred and twenty-nine days he has said that about himself and that he is not going to look for one, and that a person who wants a thing to have not worked is a person who knows what it does and there is nobody in that room who knows what it does.
  - **He did not read the writing round the edge of the wax for the third time in six days and said he was not going to say it again that week.**
  - **He did not volunteer to look at the nineteen shapes and said why: he is the only person in this city who can find a thing, and a man who is the only person who can find something ought to be very careful about how often he says that he found it. He also said that a sentence about arriving is not a Crown name and is in no book that office keeps, and that he checked that in about four seconds on the day the boy said it.**
  - **He read a date off a door in chalk, four feet back, in a village, and said nothing about it, and it is the only document of any kind he touched that day, and a chalk mark is not a document.**
  - **He said a page with a person at the top of it can be argued with, which is the protection, and a page nobody can be angry at is a page nobody can be stopped by, which is the hole, and he did not resolve the difference and neither did anybody else.**
  - **He said the answer to a boy of nine's six words is on the slate because there is nowhere else and not because it is safe, and that he is not going to say it again in a room, because a thing said twice in a room has an account.**
  - **He entered the other half of the cost of a refusal given on the thirty-seventh day:** that in about five weeks the account of a page that ends a document is going to be whatever an office nine hundred miles away says it is, and a page an office says about a page is a page about a district, and a district that is removed is exactly the thing that office would like.
- **The cost of the reading fell on people who are not him, and it fell four times, and no chapter in this batch rescued him from being a column and no chapter produced an instrument that would let him stop reading.**
- **Voice:** no new slip in this batch. He has now entered the same refusal five times in twenty days across three batches and the repetition is the method and not a tic.
- **Where he is:** Saltwake, **day 130, the Wednesday, the fortieth day of the month after this one, at about the ninth hour of the evening, in a room over a market, entered as a column, having refused the only thing anybody had that could have made it worse.**

## Sera Vail — 50, Lantern Concord notary, twenty-six years

- **Her entry that she would like to be asked before the box is opened, in a room, in daylight, with a date on it, by a person, has now been carried for five days and she has been asked five times and has answered none of the five.** On the thirty-first day of the month after this one the fifth asking was written out on a slate **and not delivered**, and a woman of twenty-four refused the delivering of it in about four seconds, and the reason is on the page: **a person who has entered that she does not know has a thing to protect, and every question put to her this week is a hand on it.** Nobody has asked her a sixth time and nobody is going to this week. The five lines exist: they were read out in a room over a market in the local language with about nine people in it so that they are on a slate and in a book that cannot travel and not in a room.
- **On the thirty-eighth day she entered three days later that she had noticed the finding of a kitchen at Nine Elms and entered that she noticed.**
- **On the thirty-ninth day of the month after this one she put her hand flat on a table and stopped a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf from putting the fifth clause of the notice of the twenty-eighth day of this flood next to a cost column in a table in a coat that says four hundred people were rehoused. She said that the line in the stone with **twelve** names on it is a count and not a debt and that it is a count of the seven stones nobody had looked at and not a count of forty-one people who could not pay. *This entry said eleven and the page in Chapter 0129 said eleven; both are now twelve, as the page has said since Chapter 0095. See `reviews/volume-03/batch-0003.md` finding 4.* and not a debt, that it is a count of the seven stones nobody had looked at and not a count of forty-one people who could not pay, and that she is fifty and has been a notary for twenty-six years and is not going to be in a room where it is.** Nobody touched the table. **This is her fifth noticing in a hundred and thirty days and she still says it is not modesty.**
- **She still holds page forty-one. Nobody has mentioned it.**

## Ivenn Marrow — 41, Crown Law scholar from Aurel, unaccused

- **Had one scene in this batch, on the thirty-ninth day of the month after this one, in a converted salt store, and he was useful in it.** He said in about nine seconds that his office has no name for a document that is in a room and not in a case: it is not a document, is not a refusal and is not a compliance, it is not on any form that office keeps, and there is a column for a document and a column for a refusal and **there is nothing at all for a thing that was written down in front of nine people and stayed in the room**, and about four hundred years of that office have run on the two columns and one was added in about a hundred and twenty years. He then said that it is the only thing in this city his office cannot act on, that he has been trying since about the fourteenth week of this flood to find a way to act on it and has not found one, and asked the room to notice that he is the man whose whole life has been finding ways to act on things and he has failed at it, and that that is not modesty either.
- **Nobody asked him to explain either name and he said neither of them.**
- **The Hattie Park counter is written once, in a book on a trestle, in his own hand, dated, and not read out, and it is eighty-nine, and it is counted from the fortieth day of this flood and from nothing else. He has still not mentioned the letter to Hattie Park and the count is spoken zero times in this batch.** It was eighty-one at day 121 and ninety at day 130.
- He said nothing wrong in a hundred and twenty-nine days, is not convicted of anything and is not going to be.
- **His room is on the hundred and eighth day of the month after this one, which is the hundred and ninety-eighth day of the flood — about eleven weeks off at Chapter 0121 and about ten weeks at Chapter 0130 — and it is not due.**

## Renna Ord — 58, boat-coat dock worker, cannot read

- **Said she is not going up the stair to the second floor again**, in about nine words, at the fourth hour of the afternoon of the thirty-first day of the month after this one: she has been up that stair three times in five days, she has not touched it, and **every time she goes up it somebody writes down where she was standing, and she cannot read, and she does not know what it says about her, and a thing she cannot read about herself is a thing about somebody else.** Nobody asked her why, and a man of about forty-four who keeps a stall wrote it in a column in his own book, and the word *herself* is in it.
- **Stood at the near gate on the thirty-second day of the month after this one, had the decision not to carry a word to the foreman read out to her, and said eleven words: *you have just written down that we are not telling him*.** A girl of seventeen counted them and the room did not check her count.
- **On the thirty-sixth day of the month after this one she was at the top of two hundred steps and had the four words *mind the low stone* read out to her twice and said nothing about them**, and on the fifth hour of the afternoon she put her hand flat on the slate with the nineteen shapes on it and said nothing at all.
- **On the thirty-eighth day, in a kitchen at Nine Elms, she had the question read out to her twice.**
- **She is the one who holds one of the four slates, and the nineteen shapes on it are hers, and on the fortieth day of the month after this one a man of thirty-two entered that she has said all nineteen of them out loud to four different people over about five weeks and that nobody has checked the count and nobody is going to.**

## Nyla Ferrow — 70, keeps the lock chapel

- **Was not asked anything in this batch, on the thirty-fifth day of the month after this one, and nobody went near the chapel.** She had said no to being the number in four seconds on the twenty-fifth day of the month after this one and the reason was that if the number is a person then the number can be asked whether the number is right, and in about twenty-nine days there is going to be a room nine hundred miles away with people in it who will be asking somebody.
- **Nobody may ask her again about being asked to be anything.** A woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf entered the reason correctly, in these words: *because she asked to be left alone and not because we thought of it*, and said that an instrument for asking has to work on everybody, and that an instrument with one house it may not go to is a favour, and a favour is a thing that stops.
- **She read the join-line on the thirty-fourth day of the month after this one, said she is at the same line as both of the others, and said she has said the number out loud twice that day and has not been asked to.**

## The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf

- **THE BATCH TURNS ON HER IN TWO PLACES AND SHE IS THE PERSON WHO PAID FOR THE BATCH'S SYSTEM MESSAGE.** On the thirty-first day of the month after this one, in a chair on a landing, she said in about four seconds that a chair is a page nobody can be angry at and the only thing in this city allowed to be nothing, and refused in about four seconds to let a man of thirty-two put a date on it. On the thirty-second day she stopped a man of about forty-four who keeps a stall from proposing that somebody carry a word to a foreman of a day crew, and said why in about nineteen seconds: a word is a thing that travels along a road, a sentence about a man is a description, a description is a removal with a column for where the person is, and **she is not going to be the reason a foreman of a day crew is a line.** On the thirty-third day she said she was glad a girl of seventeen had put a name on a page and said why, and let a woman of twenty-four look at her as though she had taken something off her.
- **On the thirty-seventh day of the month after this one, at about the fourth hour and nine minutes of the afternoon, the thirty-first thing the wall has said came up in a room with no key in it, and she said no in about four seconds, out loud, in the local language, and refused to be made the account of a page that ends a document.** She then named the cost in her own words and had it entered: that she has said out loud in front of about nine people that she knows what a page that ends a document is, and a person who knows that is a column, and saying a thing out loud in a room cannot be put back in a drawer; and that she knew the cost before she said it. **The count of refusals in the second book a clerk of the Registry keeps at home went from twenty-three to twenty-four, and the count at Aurel is thirteen, and the difference is eleven, and it has never been made smaller.**
- **On the thirty-eighth day she asked a woman of seventy of Nine Elms, in a kitchen, in daylight, on a day the woman of seventy did not choose, what a person's fourth refusal is** — and said before anybody had got their coats off that she had been the person who says the hard thing for a hundred and twenty-eight days and had never been asked whether she wanted to be that, and that she did. **She is the person who has now been glad about a page for the first time in a hundred and thirty days and has said she is not going to be glad about it in about eight weeks.**
- **On the thirty-ninth day she nearly put the fifth clause of the notice of the twenty-eighth day of this flood next to a cost column in a table in a coat, and a notary of fifty put her hand flat on a table and stopped it, and she said it was a count and asked for it entered that she was about to do it and that the reason she did not is that a notary put her hand on a table.** Nobody in that room answered the clause.
- **On the thirty-first day she said she wanted the reason a keeper of about seventy was not asked to be written down as *because she asked to be left alone and not because we thought of it*, and it is on a page in a book in this city.**
- **She is on nothing and is named on nothing and is the person who is on nothing in a room because a right of refusal that cannot be bought out is the only rule anybody in this city has brought that nobody is going to check.**

## The girl of seventeen

- **PUT HER OWN NAME AT THE TOP OF A PAGE ON THE THIRTY-THIRD DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE, IN A ROOM OVER A MARKET, IN DAYLIGHT, WITH ABOUT NINE PEOPLE IN IT, and it is the first self-naming in a hundred and twenty-three days and the cost fell on her and not on anybody else, and she said the cost is me in about four seconds.** Nobody asked her to. The page is a nineteen-line description of a box, it is local, it is not a roll, it cannot travel and it is not going into a case, and she is on no roll and is described by no form.
- **Was asked to read her own page out loud in the local language and did not, because a man of thirty-two who is the only person in this city who could have read it said no in about four seconds instead, and she read it out herself, badly, in about four minutes, and nobody corrected her and nobody read it after her.**
- **Answered a notary's four-word question — *what is this about* — in about four seconds, about herself and not about the box, in seven words, and nobody counted it, and she entered that she had wanted to count it and had not, and that the not-counting was hers and not the room's.** It is the first answer that question has had.
- **REFUSED IN UNDER A SECOND ON THE THIRTY-FIFTH DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE TO BE THE ONE WHO GOES AND ASKS THE NINE**, and it is the first time in a hundred and twenty-five days she has refused a thing she wanted to do, and the reason is that a woman with nine answers and no roll is a carrier. **She then went to her mother's kitchen and to eight kitchens by the thirty-eighth and to nine by the fortieth, and has written nothing down about any of them.**
- **Refused for the fifth time in a hundred and twenty-five days to put a name on a page, in under a second, when a man of thirty-two asked her to put one on the page his reasons were entered on, and it is entered six times that she has said no in under a second and nobody has improved on any of the six.**
- **Counted four words in this batch and the room did not check the count: five for *Has anybody read it back* was not counted by her, eleven for a docker of fifty-eight's sentence at a gate, and she wanted to say out loud how old the writing on a boy's slate was and did not.**
- **She asked a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf for the reason a keeper of about seventy was not asked to be, and got the right reason, and that reason is on a page with a date on it.**
- **She is on no roll, and has not been asked one question about being on no roll in a hundred and thirty days, and the fact that she has put her own name on a page is now a fact about a person on no roll.**
- **On the fortieth day of the month after this one she said afterwards that she had wanted to turn a blank slate over and had not, and that the wanting was hers.**

## Teodor Blaz — 31, of the second list, right hand that will not close

- **Came down two hundred steps of a river wall on the thirty-fourth day of the month after this one with a gauge in the crook of his left arm, having not been up there before, and said why in about nine seconds: a man of fifty had said in a yard nine days ago that he would not read the twenty-fifth alone, and a man of fifty who says that is a man expecting to be asked for help by somebody he has never asked.** Nobody asked him. His hand has not made a fist since the twenty-fifth day of this flood, and that is ninety-nine days.
- **Said that a man who cannot hold a thing is still the man holding it, and that the reason nobody in this city has ever asked him about that is that he is not a page and a page is the only shape anybody here knows how to be responsible for, and asked for it entered that he has said the same thing in exactly the same words four times in a hundred and twenty-four days.**
- **Said a person who can read is a person who can be asked whether the number is right, and a person who cannot read is a person who is asked whether it has moved, and that that question has an answer that is not a number, and that both of those are the reason the four are four and he is not one of them.**
- **Said *I do not know*, in about four seconds and without being asked, in answer to a man of about thirty-nine who asked out loud whether the four can make a rule about a man, and a girl of seventeen did not count the four words because nobody counts them any more, and about four people understood in about four seconds that the sentence had not been about the four and nobody said so.**

## Hettis Roke — 44, Hearthguard of the outer ward, nineteen years in

- **Stood on a landing at the top of two hundred steps of a river wall in the wrong weather for about forty minutes on the thirty-fourth day of the month after this one and was not asked anything by anybody, and it is entered with the date on it, and it is the fourteenth day in a row, and nobody in that place said so to him.** **This is the fourteenth day and about four people in that place wanted to and did not, and nobody may ask him about his own eight until he is asked properly, in a room, on a different day.** He said **I do not know** on the nineteenth day of the month after this one and that is four words and nobody improved on it and nobody turned it into a yes.
- **He has now gone twenty days without anybody in this city asking him anything about his own eight, in a room, on a different day, and that is the correct number of times and it is the only unpaid debt in this book that is about a person and not about a page.**

## Ilda Marsh — 70, of Nine Elms

- **WAS ASKED, ON THE THIRTY-EIGHTH DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE, IN A KITCHEN, IN DAYLIGHT, ON A DAY SHE DID NOT CHOOSE, WHAT A PERSON'S FOURTH REFUSAL IS, and she answered it, and it is the whole of what the thirty-eighth day of the month after this one is for.**
- **Her answer, in her own words, and nobody improved on any of it:** the first three were not hers; three times the village said no and the no belonged to nine households and nobody could take it away from anybody, and she has been thinking about the other half of that for sixteen days and nobody in this city has asked her; she has never been asked what the first three were, and she has not asked her own house or any of the eight other houses, and she has been seventy for a while and has had three chances. **The first three were refusals of a document — a notice, a date, a channel, a term sheet — and the no went into the same thing as the notice. The fourth is a refusal of a person, and there is no document, and there is a mark in chalk on nine doors that nobody in this city can wash off and nobody can explain, and that is all that is under it. It is the strongest one and it is held up by luck, and a refusal with a document under it can be un-refused by a person who reads the document, and a refusal with nothing under it can be un-refused by anybody who never heard it, and in about two months there is going to be a piece of paper in a building nine hundred miles away that says the village of nine households agreed to something and there will be nothing anywhere that says she said no. That is not a protection. That is a woman of seventy and a mark on a door.**
- **She asked for the one thing nobody in that kitchen had come for: to be put on something that is not a document, in a room, with a date on it, with what she said in it, and with the fact that there is no document under the fourth and that the first three were not hers.** It is the first time in a hundred and twenty-eight days that anybody in this city has asked to be put on a page, and it is on a page in a book in this city.
- **She refused to say why she has not asked the other eight houses, and said that in a room.**
- **She said the fourth channel has been shut for sixteen days and the water has not been received for fifty-nine days and the quarter is gone and the undertaking that would have paid for it is void and no office has been told, and that the only instrument in four hundred miles that could have asked that village a question is one that forty-nine people answered and ninety did not, forty-one of them because they could not afford the refusal, and that she is seventy and does not know what that is.**
- **She is a different person from a woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms and the two were never put in one sentence, and Chapter 0128 says so on the page.**
- **Her fourth refusal is now on a public roll in one line with no name on it**, and the only other record of it in four hundred miles is a date in chalk on nine doors.

## Elsa Skell — 44, of Corrow

- **She was not asked anything in this batch and she was not in any of the ten chapters.** What changed is the page she is on: **the second page of a stone nine miles up a road says she is owed a figure on the end of a cost column and a decision about ninety-one acres, and on the thirtieth day of the month after this one a rule under the eleventh line gave her both without asking her, and the page is now wrong and cannot be corrected, and the only correction available to anybody in four hundred miles is to ask the keeping household to take the page down on a date.** She was not asked about it and nobody went to her.
- **She is still owed a figure and a decision on a page in a stone nine miles up a road, and the page is now out of date about her, and nobody may correct it, and that is the first time the thing she is owed has been settled by something other than a person and the record of it has not been able to follow.**
- The four who read the join-line are still four, and the sixth disagreement was hers and it was about the edge and not the number, and it stands with the other five.

## Peth Lomas — 50, a mason of Saltwake, not of the crew

- **Said on the thirty-fourth day of the month after this one that the sixth disagreement was not about the number, and that he has said for ten days that it was not about the number and nobody has written down which of the six was his, and that he has never claimed one and is not going to**, and that a man of fifty who says the reading is a sixteenth under five times out of six is telling you the number and telling you nothing about the edge, and those are two different pieces of work and he does them both. **He is still under five times out of six.** The twenty-fifth reading was not taken and one ruled line is still ruled and still empty.

## Garret Slee — 61, who has copied other men's sentences for nineteen years

- **Was not in this batch and said nothing.** A sentence he had ready for two days on a road is entered on the fortieth day of the month after this one as a thing that was never said, in a list of nine things this city has that are not anywhere.
- Has still not been thanked for the nineteen stones and has still said not one word about the ground behind the ordinary wall.

## Mina Okoye — of the second list

- **Said nothing in this batch and her sentence was not used.** It has been used twice in the book, both times by her, both times about somebody who is not her.

## Peth Lammay — 24, a carrier of the second table, eleven years

- **Was not in this batch and said nothing.** Her sentence from the twenty-third day of the month after this one — a case with a name in it can be carried — was used by a girl of seventeen in a kitchen on the thirty-fifth day as the reason she may not be the one who asks, and that use is entered and attributed to her on the page and not claimed as the chapter's own.

## Tam Crane — 9

- **His answer is EIGHT DAYS OLD at Chapter 0126 and the question he asked is SEVENTY-SIX DAYS old, and the sixty-eight days is the number everybody in this city knows and the eight days is the number nobody is keeping.** It is not a lesson, it is not an instruction, it settles nothing, he was not thanked and did not expect to be, and a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf asked for it entered on the twenty-eighth day of the month after this one that she is not going to use it in about eight weeks and is not going to let anybody else use it, and a woman of twenty-four entered in advance the shame of being the person who would.
- **The two words in his second block are in ordinary use in about nine rooms in this city and are not a name, and the reason is that a name is a thing two people have said in one room, and no two of those nine rooms have met.** A woman of twenty-four and a docker of fifty-eight who cannot read found out in about nineteen seconds on the thirty-sixth day that they had both been saying them, and neither said the other one first, and it is entered that they are going to do nothing about it on purpose.
- **He was asked one question in this batch, at about the fifth hour of the afternoon of the thirty-sixth day of the month after this one, in the wrong weather, with about nine people standing about, and it was five words and it was not about the players and it was not about the arriving: *has anybody read it back.* He said one word and then nothing else. It is entered that he said nothing about having been asked, and that nobody in that place said anything to him about having been asked, and that a woman of twenty-four wanted to say out loud how old the writing on the slate was and did not, and that a docker of fifty-eight who cannot read put her hand flat on the slate with the nineteen shapes on it and said nothing at all.**
- **His four slates are still four and are still not interchangeable.** One of the four is nineteen shapes and belongs to a docker of fifty-eight who cannot read and is not repaid and is on no roll; the other three are his; the twenty-eight words are on the flat of one of the three and it has nothing on the other side; and the six words he asked on the sixth mile are on the back of another of the three, in his own hand, and there is nothing on the front of that one at all. **On the fortieth day of the month after this one he put that blank-fronted one on a bench in a room over a market and left it, and nobody turned it over.**
