- **The six words have been answered and the answer is on the slate and in no book and has nowhere to go**, and a man of thirty-two said in a room that he is not going to say it again because a thing said twice in a room has an account.

## Tamsin Quill — anchorwright, keeper of the public register of business

- **STANDS IN A ROOM FOR THE FIRST TIME IN SEVENTY-NINE DAYS, in a converted salt store on the thirty-ninth day of the month after this one, in front of about forty people, and nobody sent for her.** She writes the roll every week in that room and has stood in about nine rooms in a hundred and twenty-nine days and in two of them, a gate and a floor. **A person writing in a room and a person standing in a room are not the same thing and she has been insisting on that distinction for a hundred and twenty-nine days without once having had to stand in one.**
- **She brought the eleven-line table she has carried privately in a coat for seven years and did not read it out**, and gave her reason: a person who reads a table out in a room in the local language has made the table the account of the thing, and the account travels, and a case goes down this river in about five weeks. **She said line one is the act in the heading of the boundary page a woman of seventy carried into a room on the twenty-ninth day of the month after this one, and that its cost column says the bottom field failed in that year and four hundred people were rehoused and the boundary was not moved again, and that line seven is a cost column about the Reed margin and the villages that take water from it, and that the other nine are not hers to read and she is not going to.**
- **THE THING SHE HAS BEEN NOT SAYING FOR EIGHT DAYS, AND IT IS NOT ABOUT A MAN.** She is on a roll, and her name is on the front of the public register of this city in the second line and has been for about twenty years. A Crown district form about a public act was carried into a room on the twenty-ninth day of the month after this one and given to a girl of seventeen and not to anybody else, and she was not offered it and was not in the room, **and both of those were correct, and the finding is not that anybody was rude to her. It is that she has been holding a document about that act out of a book for seven years on purpose, and keeping it out of a book is the single most consequential thing she has done in seven years, and it is why a woman of seventy had to carry a page down a road in a cart herself.** She said that she has not come to hand anybody a document, or to be forgiven a thing nobody accused her of, or to apologise in a room to a man who did not ask, **and that a speech about somebody is a thing she does not do.**
- **She was asked one question by a man of thirty-two, about whether the other nine lines are counts or places where a number went in, and she answered it, and she told him he had asked it in about four seconds and in the wrong order, after the reason and not before it, and said that that is a finding about him and not about her, and a girl of seventeen wrote both halves down and kept them apart.**
- **She wrote one line on the roll of public business in her own hand at about the seventh hour of the evening and read it out in the local language, and it has no name on it and a count in it and no list, and it says that a village of nine households refused a fourth time as a person and not as a village, that the fourth refusal has no document under it, that the only record of it in four hundred miles is a date in chalk on nine doors, and that nine is a count and not a list.** A girl of seventeen copied it.
- **She said she had been able to say, in about four seconds on the second day of this flood, that a wall in a room is read by about forty people and is not a column and a column goes in a case, to a man who did not know what it meant, and had not said it since because nobody had asked, and that that is the whole of the seventy-nine days.**
- **She is publicly useful, she is unaccused, she is not on a hearing list, and nothing in Chapters 121–130 is a reconciliation and nothing in Chapters 121–130 is a speech about Adrian Vale.**

## A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall

- **Read the cost column and the slates and the counting aloud all ten days and is in seven of the ten chapters, and he has not slept in any of them.** In this batch he read out what the girl of seventeen had done and then said he was not going to say the thing he was going to say and asked for the not-saying to be entered. He wrote the decision not to carry a word to a foreman down on a slate. He proposed sending a word to the foreman and was refused in about nineteen seconds. He asked whether the fourth could make a rule about a man and got an answer nobody in the room had expected. He said the answer to a boy's six words had existed since the twentieth day of the month after this one and that he had been wrong in three rooms.
- **He is a different man from a man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, and no chapter in this batch put them in one sentence.**

## A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board

- **Asked out loud, in a room, on a day he did not choose, whether the four who read the join-line can make a rule about a man**, in about four seconds, and it is the only question anybody in this city has asked about that. He was refused an answer by a man of thirty-one who said **I do not know** without being asked.
- **He looked at the four words on a slate for about nine seconds and then asked a different question, and about four people in that place thought he had looked at the four words to decide whether to put a name on them, and he had not, and he never said so.**
- **He was refused once, in about four seconds, on the twenty-third day of the month after this one, by the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf, who is on nothing and is named on nothing, and the nineteen words are hers and not his.** That was in Batch 0002, not this one, and Chapter 0123 of this batch had him in a sentence with a second and phantom man of about thirty-nine who does not exist; there is one man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board and he is named in this file once. **He did not argue with a girl of seventeen's page in this batch and was not refused by her.**

## A man of sixty-eight who has carried nine cases in his life

- **Was not in this batch and said nothing.** The box he read the face of is still upstairs with its strap untied and the writing round the edge of its wax unread by anybody, and one man in this city could have read it and did not, for the third time in six days.

## A woman of about sixty-one of Four Elms

- **Was at the fork on the thirty-second day of the month after this one with a barrow and said, of the three pages in a stone: three pages in a stone is a shrine, one page is a wall somebody kept, two is a mistake — and that this is her sentence and she was not going to improve on it — and then said the thing that is the standing flag on the archive of this city: nobody has asked that household anything, because asking is how you find out what a household thinks, and a household that thinks is a household that can say no, and if it says no to one of the three pages then the page is gone, and a page we cannot get back is a page we cannot check, and we would rather have a page we can never check.** She ended the scene with *that is what you want and I am sixty-one and I have a barrow and I am going to the market.*
- **She is a different person from a woman of about sixty-one who cannot read on the upper road, and the two were never put in one sentence, and the woman of Four Elms is not given a reading property anywhere in this batch.**

## The woman of twenty-four

- **Is the batch's recorder and the batch's turn is not hers, for the third batch running.** In this batch she wrote down the decision not to send a word, the sentence that nine inches of stone is not a safe place for anything, the reasons a man of thirty-two may not ask, the fact that a girl of seventeen has put her name on a page, the six rooms and the sixth question, and the ten days themselves as a list with no names on it. **She was also wrong once, out loud, and glad she was: she was going to say that the woman of about thirty-four had not thought about what refusing the thirty-first offer would cost her, and was told she had known it before she said it, and said she was glad she was wrong.** She entered that she noticed, and entered that she noticed.
- **She is frightened of a number with nobody at the top of it, and said so in a room, and the reason is that a number cannot be asked whether it will have it and if the number is eight on the day she will have written a thing about herself that she did not know she was writing.**
- **She asked a boy of nine nothing about what he thought, and wanted to say out loud how old the writing on his slate was and did not, and that is entered twice.**

## Offstage but due

- **Nora Vale, 25, of Camden.** A letter is in a tin and it is not sent and there is no road. **The other world is not named once in Chapters 121–130.** *Open, and carried.*
- **Hattie Park, of Dunedin.** **Eighty-one days at day 121 and ninety at day 130, counted from the fortieth day of this flood and from nothing else, spoken zero times in this batch and written once in a book on a trestle in Ivenn Marrow's own hand, dated, and not read out.** *Open.*
- **A woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms.** Not asked, not approached, not named in any room where it could reach her, and nobody stood outside the fourth house. **She appears in this batch twice, in a closing paragraph of Chapter 0128 as the fifth person at that village who is not present and was not asked, and in the ledger.** *Open and deliberately unasked.*
- **A man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close**, who is the only person in a room who says *I do not know* out loud without being asked, and who held a gauge in the crook of his arm on the thirty-fourth day of the month after this one and said it about himself. *Open.*
- **A man of about thirty-five whose right shoulder went on the thirty-first day of this flood, was reduced twice, cannot be set, did not go and was not made whole**, on a page in a stone as a debt with no line that will mend it. *Open, and unchanged.*
- **A woman of forty-one owed the work in her own words in the document that says the work is not written down anywhere.** *Open, and it is one of the twelve.*
- **A woman of about twenty-nine who is a different person from a boy of nine and from Curtis Okonkwo, twenty, of the second list**, and who was not in this batch and was not merged with anybody.
- **The six of the first group and the eleven of the second group** are still named, still with no land rights, no class, no standing and no workway, except one, and the one is not whole, has refused the asking once, and is owed a document that does not contain her.
- **A girl of sixteen** is one of the two people on no roll in this city, and has still not been asked about it. **The two people on no roll may never be witnesses, and a girl of seventeen who is on no roll is not one of them.**
- **Jonah Pike, 42, of the White Ledger**, was not in these ten days and has said nothing since Chapter 0102. He is not convicted of anything and is not going to be, and the seat has not been offered a sixth time.
- **Wenna Cray's fifth statement of error, four sentences, all correct, stands unanswerable and unanswered and was not answered in this batch.**
- **Elsa Skell's figure and Elsa Skell's ninety-one acres, and the season of both, and a page in a stone that is now wrong about her and cannot be corrected**, **Ilda Marsh's cart and a fourth refusal with nothing under it**, **the second list which did not go at all**, and **the four who read the join-line, who are still four.**

---

# Volume 03, the fourth ten days and the last four: Chapters 131–150

**This section is the state of every person on the page at the close of Volume 03, at day 154, the hundred and fifty-fourth day of the flood. Every entry has a date on it. It supersedes the Volume 03 Batch 0003 section above for the purposes of Volume 04 and does not undo it.**

## Adrian Vale — 32, from another world

- **STILL STAGE 2, Passage holder, in the doing and not in a ceremony. Unbound. Barred from every roster, on no roster of the licence and on no roster of the instrument of the twenty-second day of the new month, at his own request, in his own hand, with the date on it. He holds no mark of his own, no workway, no title, no land right, no class privilege and no seat. He performed no working in these twenty days, no threshold was opened, the crossing is not written in them, and the other world is not named once in Chapters 131–150.**
- **ON THE SIXTY-FOURTH DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE HE REFUSED THE APPOINTMENT OF CROWN-APPOINTED INTERPRETER WITH UNLIMITED AUTHORITY, in a room, in daylight, in the local language, with a date in chalk on the door behind him, in front of about nine people and about two hundred and forty people who could hear him, and he read his own declination out in the local language because there is no one else in the room who can.** The declination is on the page and the second eighth clause of the appointment is in it: a declining is not a refusal until it is read by the person appointed.
- **The reason was given once and was not improved on, and nobody asked him for a second version: a translation is a description, a description is a removal with a column for where the person is, and a post does not change the form, a post changes who fills in the places, and a man who is filling nine places is a column.**
- **THE REFUSAL DID NOT PRODUCE THE COMPACT, and he said so out loud and asked for it entered: the compact was on a slate on the fiftieth day of the month after this one and the post was on a table on the forty-ninth, and if he had said yes the compact would be exactly the eleven lines it is with his voice in it instead of hers.** No later chapter may present the refusal as the cause of anything.
- **A LATER OFFER THAT LOOKED LIKE THE POST AND WAS NOT THE POST WAS REFUSED IN ABOUT FOUR SECONDS FOR THE SAME REASON, AND THE REASON WAS STATED ONCE AND NOT IMPROVED ON: a reading that is the price of a place is a reading that is the price of a place, and a thing that cannot be argued with because it is a day is a chair with no date on it.** The offer was one reading, one document, one day, no standing, no roster, no seat, no wage, no mark, no title and no page, and the man who built it said it took him four hours and he could not make the places be anything else.
- **What it cost him, in his own words, in that room:** he has given up the only thing he is good at as a way of being needed; he has no office, no standing, no roster, no mark, no seat, and no office in Saltwake for what he is; the knowing does not come back and the way home goes through a room with no door and neither was coming back whether he said yes or no; **and the answer to the eighth column, when it comes in about five weeks, will be read by somebody else or by nobody, and he chose that in a room in daylight in the local language and nobody praised him for it and he asked for that to be entered before it happened.** He also said out loud, in front of about two hundred and forty people, that **a man with no mark, on no roster, outside every office, is the most findable person in this world, and that this is not a safety and he knows it.**
- **He asked that nobody call him a hero and that nobody call the man who offered the honest version a villain, and he said he was not going to be the one who said it afterwards.** Nobody thanked him and he said in advance that nobody would.
- **Other things he said in these twenty days, each of which is on the page and none of which improved on another.** On the forty-first day: a compact is a document, and a document is a description, and there are two documents and only one of them is a person, and if the two ever say different things this city will believe the one that travels. On the forty-fourth day: he was asked about the writing round the edge of the wax and **said nothing at all**, and the not-saying is the entry, and it is the fifth kind of not-doing. On the forty-sixth day: the third reason he may not ask, which is that he would be asking, and then the answers would be his. On the forty-seventh day: **three eights in four hundred miles, and a man who wants them to be one is a man who wants and not a man who knows.** On the forty-ninth day: he said the name of the shape out loud once, in the local language, and did not explain it. On the fiftieth day: he refused, in about four seconds, to read the compact, and said there is no line on that page that lets him choose the reader. On the fifty-fifth day: **he cannot go to the First Landing and said why, and that if he were going he would be the account of it before anybody else had stood on it, and a delegation with him in it is a description.** On the sixty-fourth day: the volume's question is answered in a form and it is unpaid, and he stopped after nine sentences of it that he did not say.
- **No chapter in this batch rescued him from being a column and no chapter produced an instrument that would let him stop reading. He has now refused three things he was the only person in this city able to do: a page with a girl's name on it, a wax, and a compact.**

## Tamsin Quill — 29, anchorwright, keeper of the public register of business

- **STOOD UP IN THE HEARING ROOM ON THE SIXTY-FOURTH DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE AND READ ELEVEN LINES OUT LOUD IN THE LOCAL LANGUAGE, and nobody had asked her and there was no line on the compact that let anybody choose.** She is the reader. The eighth line of the compact says the person who reads it out in a room is the account of it and is not on any roll, and she said on her feet that that clause is not against her doing it, it is a clause that says what it costs.
- **She said why, in her own words, and it is hers alone, and the page has it in the local language and the review pass put the page's words back into this file after finding an inserted clause here that is nowhere in the book: a compact nobody reads is a piece of paper, and she knows what a piece of paper is because she has kept one out of a book for seven years. It is a door. And if she does not read it out in a room in daylight with a date on it then the only account of it for four hundred years is a correct number about a district on a wall in a converted salt store, and that account has one place for a count and one place for the assent and they are the same place.**
- **The cost she named, and she named it before she read: her name is on the front of the public register of this city in the second line and has been for about twenty years, it is the only name in this city on a public page in ordinary course, she has never once been asked to read anything out loud on purpose, and about two hundred and forty people in that hall heard her say eleven lines and in about four weeks a clerk on a quay nine hundred miles away is going to write down who read them.** She is now a person who has been made the account of a thing in a room, by choosing.
- **On the forty-ninth day of the month after this one she refused the thirty-second thing the wall has said in this city, in about four seconds, out loud, in the local language, in front of about nine people including a man from that office who wrote it down.** She named the cost: that a thing said out loud in a room an office knows about is a thing that can go into a return. **That is the third registered refusal and the third time the count in the second book a clerk of the Registry keeps at home has ever moved, and it is now twenty-five.** It is also the first time she has refused a thing out loud in a room, and it is the second refusal of her life in this volume and neither of them was a reconciliation and neither was a speech about anybody.
- **She also said, on the fifty-fifth day, that the clause *to open nothing* in the delegation is hers and that she put it in herself and would like it kept as hers, because in about four months somebody is going to say that a delegation was sent to open something and she did not write that.**
- **She is publicly useful, she is unaccused, she is not on a hearing list, and nothing in Chapters 131–150 is a reconciliation, an apology, or a speech about Adrian Vale, and she said again that a speech about somebody is a thing she does not do.** Her standing with him is a mutual, public, documented check and this is the first time in three volumes that they have each done separate work with separate loyalties in front of about two hundred and forty people, and neither of them rescued the other.

## Sera Vail — 50, a notary, twenty-six years

- **ANSWERED, ON THE SIXTY-FOURTH DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE, IN A ROOM, IN DAYLIGHT, WITH A DATE ON IT, BY A PERSON, WHO CALLED HER SERA VAIL IN THE LOCAL LANGUAGE BECAUSE SHE HAD ASKED TO BE ASKED BY A PERSON.** Six askings in thirty-eight days, counted from the twenty-sixth day of the month after this one to the sixty-fourth, and thirty-three days since the fifth asking was written out on a slate on the thirty-first and not delivered. **It is the first answer she has given to anything since the twenty-sixth day of the month after this one and it is the first time in twenty-six years that the notary of about nine hundred things has been the person who said no to an opening in a room.**
- **She said no, and not to being asked: no to the opening, today, in this room. The reason is on the page in her words: a thing that is opened in a hearing is a thing the hearing is about, and after that morning the only instrument this city has with that office is the room, and it is gone in a day, and if a box is opened in it this afternoon then the room is about a box for four hundred years.**
- **She asked for three things to be entered and all three were: that the not-knowing of thirty-eight days was real and was not modesty; that she wanted to be asked; and that the thing she wanted and the thing she said are not the same thing and she did not know that that morning.** She put both hands flat on the step at the back, which is not a table.
- **The box is still shut, the strap on it is still untied, and the writing round the edge of the wax is still unread by anybody in a hundred and fifty-four days.**
- **Also in this batch: she refused an answer that four people had agreed she would give, on the forty-fourth day of the month after this one, in about four seconds, and the reason is that a supplied answer is a description of a person and she has been the description of about nine hundred people.** She read out the four lines of the twelfth shape in Chapter 0138 and said that a correct number about a district is a district that is described correctly and that nothing in that page is a lie, and that a page nobody can argue with is a page nobody can stop. She put her hand flat on a table in Chapter 0141 to stop a woman of about thirty-four from putting the fifth clause of the notice of the twenty-eighth day of this flood next to a cost column. She said in Chapter 0140 that a notary of fifty has never in twenty-six years had a compact with a person in it.
- **She still holds page forty-one. Nobody has mentioned it.**

## Ivenn Marrow — 41, Crown Law scholar from Aurel, unaccused

- **PUT AN INSTRUMENT ON A TABLE IN THIS BATCH BECAUSE HE WAS REQUIRED TO, AND SAID SO, AND ASKED FOR IT TO BE ENTERED THAT HE PUT IT AFTER THE MAN SAID NO AND NOT BEFORE.** The thirteenth shape, dated the fortieth day of the month after this one, nine terms, of which the fifth is the only one anybody in that room wrote, and of which there are two eighths: *the person appointed is not to be asked whether he accepts it* and *a declining is not a refusal until it is read by the person appointed*. He said he had had it since Friday morning, that he did not like it, and that the room should notice that the man who has spent nineteen days failing to act on something has just been handed the means and that the means is a person.
- **HE BUILT THE BEST VERSION OF THE THING THAT WAS REFUSED, in about four hours, out of everything that office does not do: one reading, one document, one day, in a room, in daylight, with a date on it, no standing, no roster, no seat, no wage, no mark, no title, no page.** He was refused in about four seconds and he said the reason back, *it is a day*, and did not argue.
- **HE COUNTED WHICH OF THE ELEVEN LINES OF THE COMPACT HIS OFFICE CAN ACT ON, in about forty minutes, in a room with no key in it, and it is four — two, three, seven and nine, a count, an assent, a household and an ending — and six it cannot, and one of the six he wants. He said the reason he cannot act on the fifth line is that his office revokes things and does not receive things that stop by themselves, and that the form for an ending has a compulsory reason in it, and that he has been the man who fills in the page about a person for nineteen days.**
- **HE DID NOT DRAFT THE COMPACT, and said that if he had, the eleventh shape would have become a Crown instrument, and asked for it to be entered in advance that he would lie about having never seen it.**
- **HE READ FOUR LINES OF THE RECOGNITION OUT IN THE LOCAL LANGUAGE AS HIMSELF, said that nobody in his office had told him to read it, and that it is in no book, and that he had looked. He said out loud that a provisional recognition is a revocable one, and that a district recognised provisionally is a district that can be described as not recognised, and that there is a form for that too and he has not seen it in a hundred and fifty-four days and would rather say so now.** He said the sentence about his office having no name for a document that is in a room and not in a case into a hall, in daylight, in the local language, on the sixty-first day of the month after this one, before a clerk of the fourth desk was asked to be in a room with them, and not after.
- **HE CONFIRMED NEITHER NAME AND EXPLAINED NEITHER, when a man of thirty-two said one of them out loud, and said he had said those two words in a room in this city on a named day and put them in his own hand in a book and had been asked to explain them twice since and had not, and that he had just declined to be useful in the one room in four hundred miles where he was the only person who could have been.**
- **The Hattie Park counter is written once, in a book on a trestle, in his own hand, dated, and not read out, and it is spoken zero times in this batch, and a woman of twenty-four said the word number and not the figure and was right. It is ninety-one at day 131 and one hundred and fourteen on day 154, counted from the fortieth day of this flood and from nothing else, and he has still not mentioned the letter.**
- He said nothing wrong in a hundred and fifty-four days, is not convicted of anything and is not going to be. **His room is on the hundred and eighth day of the month after this one, which is day 198, which is about six weeks off on the hearing day, and it is not due and it was not brought forward.**

## Renna Ord — 58, boat-coat dock worker, cannot read

- **SAID NINE WORDS ON A QUAY ON THE FORTY-SECOND DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE, and nobody wrote them down, and the reason nobody wrote them down is that nobody could have put it on a page without a hand and a date and there was no slate anybody could read on a quay in that wind. The nine words are on the page: *nobody asked them to come, nobody can stop them, we are just standing here.* And then, after about four seconds, in case anybody had not understood the first part: *that is a good thing to be.***
- **CAME UP NINE MILES WITH A BARROW AND A GIRL OF SEVENTEEN BECAUSE THE BARROW WAS GOING THAT WAY, and said no in about four seconds when asked whether she minded, and gave the reason that the barrow was heavy and two more hands on it are two more hands and she has not had anybody help her with a barrow in about five weeks. And then said, out loud for the first time in a hundred and forty-two days, that she would go up nine miles twice for a wedding anyway.**
- **SAID NINE WORDS ABOUT A BOY'S SLATE ON THE FIFTY-SECOND DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE, and a girl of seventeen counted them and nobody checked her count, and they are the finding of the chapter: that is the only page in this city with an address on it.**
- **AND THEN, TWO THIRDS OF THE WAY DOWN THE NINE MILES, SHE TOOK A SLATE OUT OF HER POCKET AND LOOKED AT NINETEEN SHAPES ON IT THAT SHE CANNOT READ AND SAID THAT SHE WOULD LIKE SOMEBODY TO CHECK THEM AND NOT IN A BOOK, and that the instrument would be a person saying them back to her, and that it would take about four minutes and not travel and would leave a person who can read holding nineteen words of hers, and that she has thought about it for four days and is not doing it and is telling you instead.** That is the fifth thing she has said out loud in a hundred and forty-two days that she has not written down.
- She is still on no roll, is not repaid, and holds one of the four slates, and it is the only one of the four that nobody in this city can read.

## Nyla Ferrow — 70, keeps the lock chapel

- **WAS SENT FOR ON THE SIXTIETH DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE, DURING THE BUILDING OF THE HEARING ROOM, AND DID NOT COME, AND NOBODY SENT A SECOND MESSAGE.** The reason is on a slate in a book in this city and the reason is *because she asked to be left alone and not because we thought of it*. **Nobody may ask her again about being asked to be anything, and this is the second time in twenty days that the correct reason has been recorded in the correct words, and the second time nobody acted on it.**

## The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf

- **REFUSED IN ABOUT FOUR SECONDS, TWICE IN THESE TWENTY DAYS, BOTH ON A SHUTTLE STALL AND BOTH IN A GOOD MOOD.** On the forty-third day, with a girl of seventeen on the back of the shut stall: a compact is a page with a person's name in every place in it, and a person in a place where a person can be found is a person who can be stopped. **She then allowed one line of a slate to have her name against it, in about four seconds, in her own hand, and the cost of it is hers, and it is the first name she has allowed in a hundred and fifty-four days.**
- **On the forty-fifth day, under the near gate, she said the sentence that is the finding of the day: a person who carries a cost is a person who can be asked, and about four people in this city have wanted to do that for a hundred and thirty-five days, and the reason nobody has is not that we worked it out, it is that we are frightened, and we found a rule that says not to and we have been hiding behind the rule since about the fortieth day.** She went down nine steps of a condemned stair, which she had not done in a hundred and thirty-five days, and came back up and said nothing more about it.
- **On the fifty-fourth day she said the last thing of the day, and it is nine words, and nobody counted them: then the guest who asks to stop is the only person in it.** And in Chapter 0148 she refused, in about four seconds, to let a man from that office be given a bench facing the door, and the reason is that in about four months there will be a story about a bench and a person in it, and the story will be that somebody was made to sit on it. **In Chapter 0150 she said what *named* means, in a hall, to about nine people, and offered in advance that nobody was going to be read back at unless they agreed, and nothing was required of anybody.**
- **On the forty-sixth day a woman of about forty-four who keeps a stall and a man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall were not in one sentence with her in a single day, and it is entered that they were not.** She is on nothing and is named on nothing, and she is now the person who reads out the terms a place costs, and she has not been thanked for any of it.

## The girl of seventeen

- **WENT TO NINE KITCHENS AGAIN OVER TWO DAYS IN THE LAST WEEK, TOLD FOUR THINGS AND NOT A FIFTH, AND WROTE NOTHING DOWN.** The difference between the eighth household and the ninth is eight days and she said out loud that she could not get to the ninth on Saturday without going past it and that is the whole reason and it is not a reason she would put on a page, and that she is telling them anyway because about four of them worked out something on the fortieth day of the month after this one about this city not putting its rooms together.
- **PRACTISED A SENTENCE OF ELEVEN WORDS ALONE ON A ROAD AT ABOUT THE TWO-MILE MARK, OUT LOUD, TO NOBODY, TO HEAR WHETHER IT WAS THE RIGHT ELEVEN WORDS, and said out loud *a practice is a toll*, and there was nobody there and that is the reason she said it.** It is entered that a person of seventeen has been practising sentences alone on a road in this city and nobody knew and nobody is going to be told.
- **REFUSED TO BE THE ONE WHO GOES TO THE NINTH KITCHEN ON THE DAY, AGAIN, IN UNDER A SECOND, on the ground that a woman with nine answers and no roll is a carrier.** And then went.
- **DID NOT TURN OVER THE SLATE WITH THE TWENTY-EIGHT WORDS ON IT, and said the reason out loud in a gateway nine miles up a road and it is hers: two words in about nine rooms and none of those nine rooms has met any of the other eight, and that is the only reason they are not a name, and she read the answer out in one room on the twenty-eighth and she is not going to be the one who makes it two.** She is the one who put it there.
- **SAID ON THE FIFTY-SIXTH DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE THAT SHE WILL STAND UP IN THE HEARING, AND NOBODY WROTE IT DOWN, AND SHE SAID THAT WAS WHY SHE SAID IT.** And then: if she is standing up when eleven lines are read out then she is part of it and there is no page that says she was not asked, because she was not asked, she volunteered, and a thing a person volunteers for is a thing a person can be asked to do again. And: *I will stand up and I will say I was not asked, and if that is a description of me then it is one I chose in a kitchen and I am seventeen and I know exactly what a name at the top of a page is, and I have not been able to decide for eleven days which of those it is going to be, and I have decided to find out in a room rather than in a book.* **And then she said the best sentence anybody said in that kitchen and asked not to be thanked for saying it, because she would rather nobody said anything at all.**
- **DREW THE ELEVEN LINES OF THE COMPACT, in about forty minutes, in her own hand, and read them back once to herself and did not read them out, and it is entered that she is the same person who has written down every decision this city has made since the second day of the flood and that nobody had noticed until a notary said it out loud.**
- **READ THE WHOLE COMPACT BACK TWICE IN THE HALL ON THE HEARING DAY, FOR THE PEOPLE AT THE BACK, FROM A SLATE AND NOT FROM HER OWN PAGE, because her own has her name at the top of it and she would not put the two in a hall together.** About nine people said their own names out loud in that room and she wrote none of them down.
- **DID NOT MOVE THE CHAIR ON THE LANDING, and said she would rather it stay a chair, because in about four months there will be a story about a chair and a person in it and the story will be that a girl of seventeen moved a chair.**
- She is on no roll, has been for a hundred and fifty-four days, and has still not been asked about it.

## Hettis Roke — 44, Hearthguard of the outer ward, nineteen years in

- **WAS ASKED, IN A ROOM, IN DAYLIGHT, WITH A DATE ON IT, ON THE FORTY-SEVENTH DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE, AFTER TWENTY-SEVEN DAYS ON WHICH NOBODY IN THIS CITY ASKED HIM ANYTHING ABOUT HIS OWN EIGHT, and he had asked for it on the thirtieth day of the new month, a hundred and thirty-seven days earlier, and the count is the twenty-seventh day counted from the twenty-first day of the month after this one, which was the hundred and eleventh day of this flood.**
- **THE ASKING WAS DONE PROPERLY AND IT TOOK FOUR DAYS TO BUILD THE INSTRUMENT: the question in his own words, written out by a girl of seventeen who copies things and is on no roll, given to every person who would be in the room four days before the day, and anybody who could not be given it was not going to be in the room; a room with about nine chairs; a date on a slate; and nobody asked him whether the day suited him.** He came off duty, nineteen minutes late, and nobody said anything about the nineteen minutes.
- **HE SAID NO IN ABOUT FOUR SECONDS AND IT IS THE SAME NO.** It will stand for about four years. He gave the same forty seconds in the same words as the yard, and gave the reason for the difference in his own words: there are nine people in this room who were each given the question four days ago and one of them can be asked whether the asking was proper, and nobody at the end of a yard can be.
- **HE ASKED, BEFORE HE WENT, THAT HE WAS NOT GOING TO BE THANKED, AND SAID THAT IF ANYBODY THANKED HIM HE WOULD HAVE TO THINK ABOUT IT FOR A WEEK. Nobody thanked him. The count of days is twenty-seven and it is not going to be counted again.** About four people in that room wanted to thank him and did not.
- **The unpaid debt in this book that is about a person and not about a page is paid. It is closed.**

## Ilda Marsh — 70, of Nine Elms

- **Was not in any of the twenty days and was not asked anything and nobody went to her village.** The fourth refusal is **forty-two days old at day 154**, the water has not been received for eighty-five days counted from the sixty-ninth day of this flood, the fourth line of a term sheet is in force, and the undertaking that would have paid for it is void and no office has been told.
- **And there is now a page about the fourth refusal, on the back of a stone nine miles up a road, which does not name her village.** She asked for it on the thirty-eighth day of the month after this one and it went in on the fifty-third, and a woman of seventy said that somebody would have to ask and that whoever asked it would be a person and not a village and she would be glad of that and she would be watching, and somebody asked.
- **She is a different person from a woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at that village and the two were never put in one sentence, and Chapter 0150 says so on the page.**

## Elsa Skell — 44, of Corrow

- **Was not in any of the twenty days and was not asked about the page in the stone that is wrong about her.** The keeping household of that stone was asked, on the fifty-third day of the month after this one, whether it would take the page down, and **they said no, and the reason is that a stone that has had a page taken down is a stone that can be corrected and a stone that can be corrected is a stone that will be corrected by whoever comes next.** The page stays wrong on purpose.
- **And what the woman who keeps it said, in her own words, is that the only thing she can offer a woman of forty-four of Corrow is that she will not take it down, she will not ask her, and she will have to look at it every time she comes up the road for the next forty years.** That is the strangest protection in this book and it is not safe and nobody pretends it is.
- The four who read the join-line are still four, and the sixth disagreement was hers and it was about the edge and not the number, and it stands with the other five. **The twenty-fifth reading has not been taken for sixty-nine days.**

## Peth Lomas — 50, a mason of Saltwake, not of the crew

- **Was not in any of the twenty days and said nothing. The twenty-fifth reading was not taken on any of them and one ruled line is still ruled and still empty, and the four who read the join-line did not disagree again.** He is still under five times out of six.

## Garret Slee — 61, who has copied other men's sentences for nineteen years

- **READ FOUR LINES OUT LOUD IN THE LOCAL LANGUAGE IN A ROOM FOR THE FIRST TIME IN HIS LIFE, on the forty-fourth day of the month after this one, and read it twice and the second time was slower.** He had been asked four times in nineteen years and had never once been asked, and he said so, and he said he had had a sentence of his own ready for about two days of his life in a road and never said it and nobody ever found out, **and he asked for both of those to be entered and that they are the same shape.** A woman of twenty-four wanted to ask him about the sentence and did not, and the reason she gave afterwards is that a sentence a man has had ready for two days is the only thing he owns that nobody in this city can take off him.
- He has still not been thanked for the nineteen stones and has still said not one word about the ground behind the ordinary wall.

## Mina Okoye — of the second list

- **Said nothing in this batch and her sentence was not used.** It has been used twice in the book, both times by her.

## Peth Lammay — 24, a carrier of the second table, eleven years

- **Said the thing that closed the question of whether a person could be brought up the river, on the forty-second day of the month after this one, in four seconds, on a quay: the Open Hand will not take a person, because carrying a person means putting a person in a carrier's book, and a book of names is a roll, and a roll goes in a case, and she has not been able to make that sentence come out different once in eleven years.** She walked down from the chandlery before anybody sent for her because she works on the same quay on a Sunday.
- **And then she said that the right of refusal cannot be bought out, and that that is the only rule anybody has brought this city in a hundred and thirty-four days that nobody is going to check, and that she is standing on it, because the refusal is easy and the easy ones are the only ones worth having.** **And she did not want a person refused. She said so: she did not want the refusal to be that. She wanted the refusal to be about the book.**
- Her sentence from the twenty-third day of the month after this one — a case with a name in it can be carried — is the reason the Open Hand's refusal is the right shape, and it is entered and attributed to her and not claimed as anybody else's.

## Tam Crane — 9

- **TURNED ONE OF HIS OWN SLATES OVER ON THE FIFTY-SECOND DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE IN A YARD NINE MILES UP THE UPPER ROAD, without being asked and without any appearance of having decided to, and there was nothing on the other side.** He said, in about four seconds, *because the front is for other people*, when a girl of seventeen asked him why the front was blank, and then said that he wanted that written down and he wanted it not written down and he wanted both, and both were entered with the date on it and no name on it, and she read it back to him twice because he is nine and it is four seconds.
- **HIS ANSWER IS TWENTY-FOUR DAYS OLD AND WAS SIXTY-EIGHT DAYS LATE WHEN IT WAS WRITTEN, AND THE QUESTION IS NINETY-TWO DAYS OLD, and the three figures are on the page with the event each one is measured from named, which is the rule the Batch 0003 review left.** **The slate with the twenty-eight words on it is still face down where it has been since the twenty-eighth day of the month after this one, and the slate with the six words on it is face up on a step in a yard with nothing on it.** The twenty-eight words have now been read aloud in one room, once, on purpose, and never repeated.

## Marda Rood — about sixty-one, of Four Elms

- **ANSWERED THE QUESTION OF WHETHER THE KEEPING HOUSEHOLD OF THE STONE WOULD HOLD A FOURTH PAGE, on the fifty-third day of the month after this one, and nobody asked her whether she would answer.** She said three pages in a stone is a shrine and one is a wall somebody kept and two is a mistake, and that **she has been up that road two hours thinking about it and she does not have a word for four.**
- **She said yes, on the back of the stone, on three conditions: anybody in the keeping household may take it down on a date of its own choosing and is not asked why twice; the person who asks is not thanked, now or in four years; and the second page, which is wrong, stays as it is for as long as the stone stands.** And the reason for the third is the finding of the batch: a stone that has had a page taken down is a stone that can be corrected, and a stone that can be corrected is a stone that will be corrected by whoever comes next, and they will be doing it for a reason.
- **And she said that the reason she will not take it down, and will not ask the woman of forty-four of Corrow, and will have to look at it every time she comes up the road for the next forty years, is the only thing she has to offer her, and then she said *no* when asked to say it again, because she said it four years ago about the first page and she is not going to be a woman who says things twice on a road.**
- She is a different person from a woman of about sixty-one who cannot read on the upper road, and the two were never put in one sentence.

## The woman of twenty-four

- **IS THIS BATCH'S RECORDER AND THE BATCH'S TURN IS NOT HERS, FOR THE FOURTH BATCH RUNNING — except on one day.** On the forty-first day she said the thing that made the room stop, about a compact being a piece of paper. On the forty-third day she counted nine words and nobody checked her count. On the forty-ninth day she said a number and not a figure and was right. On the fiftieth day **she drew the eleven lines of the compact, and it is entered that nobody had noticed that she is the same person who has written down every decision this city has made since the second day of the flood until a notary said it out loud.** On the forty-sixth day she said *I am not asking and you did not say* about a number in a book on a trestle.
- **She was wrong once, out loud, on the forty-third day, and glad she was.** A woman of about thirty-four told her that the cost of refusing the thirty-second offer was known before it was said and the woman of twenty-four had been going to say she had not, and she said she was glad she was wrong. **She has still not been asked about being on no roll in a hundred and fifty-four days, and she is the person who drew a compact in a hundred and fifty-four days, and nobody said anything about either of those facts in the same room.**

## A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall

- **PROPOSED THE BEST ARGUMENT IN THE BATCH, on the fifty-sixth day of the month after this one, and took it back himself, and asked for it to be entered that he knew it was the best one.** The argument was that if a man of thirty-two is going to answer the post in a room and everybody who has to live with it will hear him, then the nine should be asked properly first, in a room, on a day they did not choose. **The reason it lost is the one a girl of seventeen gave: asking them would make nine remarks into nine answers, and nine answers is a list.**
- **He carried most of a table down a stair on the fifty-ninth day of the month after this one and looked at it in the hall on the Wednesday and said that it had been a table for about forty years and a form for about a hundred and forty-nine days, and that nobody had noticed, and that this is the only thing anybody in this city has built that has been got rid of in a hundred and forty-nine days without a fight.** He put the table back at about the seventh hour on the day of the hearing, which was the sixty-fourth day of the month after this one and there is only the one, because a room with nothing in it that nine people have been in for four hours needs a table in it for the people who come in after, and nobody agreed to that and he did it anyway.
- He asked whether the count of nine was going to be eight on the day, and was told that if it is, nobody will ever be able to say who was not in the room, **and that noticing it is a preference, and that this is the first time in a hundred and forty-nine days he has caught himself having one.** He is a different man from a man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall and from **a woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf, who is a woman and not a man and is the same person in all fifty chapters she appears in**, and no two of the three were put in one sentence in these twenty days. **A fourth time this close phase had to check which of three near-identical stall-keepers a sentence meant, and the man-form of the wharf stall-keeper that stood in this file and in `state/continuity.md` was a phantom of a one-word error in Chapter 0149 and is gone.**

## A man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board

- **SAID IN THE FIFTY-FIFTH DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE *I WILL GO*, AND THEN, IN THE SAME BREATH, THAT HE WILL NOT BE NAMED, AND ASKED FOR THE SECOND HALF TO BE WRITTEN DOWN AND NOT THE FIRST.** He has been refused by name four times in four years by the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf, on a board with nineteen rates on it and no column for a name, and he said it was not a different thing this time and he was right. **A delegation is a number with legs and the man of thirty-nine is the only person in this batch who has said both halves of that in the same sentence.**
- He was on the quay on the forty-second day and the sixty-first day in case anything came up the river, and he was the first to say *the second thing in a hundred and forty years*, and he has not been on any list and is not going to be.

## A man of sixty-eight who has carried nine cases in his life

- **SAID, ON THE FORTY-SECOND DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE, IN ABOUT NINE SECONDS AND BADLY, THAT HE WOULD CARRY A PERSON AND WOULD NOT WRITE THE HOUSEHOLD, and that is the whole of what he was saying and it took him nine seconds to say it badly.** A case has a sender and a sender is one line. A person has a household, and a household is a person, and there is no way in this world to write a household on one line, and if it is written on two then the second is a place, and a place is a column, and a column goes in a case. **He did not come further down the quay than the third step, and he said that nobody had ever asked him what he thinks about the rule on his book and he is not starting on a Sunday in that wind.** He has carried nine cases and no people and he is the ninth reason anybody in this city has given for something.
- The box he read the face of is still upstairs with its strap untied.

## A woman of about sixty-one of Four Elms

**See Marda Rood above. She is the same person and is not a second entry.** She is a different person from a woman of about sixty-one who cannot read on the upper road, and the two were never put in one sentence.

## A clerk of the fourth desk — about thirty-six

- **CAME UP THIS RIVER ON THE SIXTY-FIRST DAY OF THE MONTH AFTER THIS ONE, IN A PASSENGER PLACE, EIGHTEEN DAYS ON THE WATER, AND STEPPED OFF AT A QUAY IN FRONT OF ABOUT NINE PEOPLE WHO HAD GONE DOWN TO LOOK AT THE WATER. She is the second thing in a hundred and forty years to come up this river and the first one that is a person.** She is a clerk of the fourth desk, eleven years in the post, the ninth person to have had that desk and the fourth to have stayed on it, and she is not the office: she said so on the quay before anybody had asked.
- **SHE CAME BECAUSE A CASE WITH A QUESTION IN ITS EIGHTH COLUMN HAS BEEN ON HER DESK FOR FORTY-THREE DAYS AND SHE MAY NEITHER ASK A QUESTION ABOUT IT NOR REFUSE ONE, and the only thing she is allowed to do is go and look, and she came nine hundred miles on her own account and on somebody else's wages and notified nobody and has no authority to be here and has nothing in her bag but a coat.** She said all of that in about four minutes in a room and nobody said anything for about nineteen seconds afterwards.
- **NOBODY IN THIS CITY ASKED HER ABOUT THE EIGHTH COLUMN, and she noticed that they did not, and she asked for it to be entered about four times because she did not think anybody in that room had.** And a man of thirty-two said out loud why: if this city asks, then the question becomes a question this city has asked, and the asking is the account of it, and there is a column for where the person is.
- **She was asked, on the sixty-second day, in a room and not in a hall, whether she would be in the hearing on the sixty-fourth, and she said yes, and said it would not be thanked and would not be improved on, and she was thanked, and she asked for it not to be entered, and it was entered that she asked for it not to be entered.** She said she had been thanked for nothing by anybody in eleven years and that it had not once made her work harder, and that she would like that in a book with a date on it because it is going in a case and she will not be thanked again by that office for about nine years.
- **She is not a friend and she is not a villain. She is a person who came to look at a district and is now, from the sixty-first day of the month after this one, a public official in this city, and nobody in this city is on her side of anything, and a man of forty-one in a very good coat told her, into a hall, in the local language, that his office has no name for a document that is in a room and not in a case, before she was asked to be in a room with them, and not after.**

## Offstage but due

- **Nora Vale, 25, of Camden.** A letter is in a tin and it is not sent and there is no road. **The other world is not named once in Chapters 131–150.** *Open, and carried.*
- **Hattie Park, of Dunedin.** **Ninety-one days at day 131 and one hundred and fourteen on day 154, counted from the fortieth day of this flood and from nothing else, spoken zero times in this batch and written once in a book on a trestle in Ivenn Marrow's own hand, dated, and not read out.** *Open, and his room is about six weeks off and is not due.*
- **A woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms.** Not asked, not approached, not named in any room where it could reach her, and nobody stood outside the fourth house. **She appears in this batch twice, both times in the closing coda of Chapter 0150 and not in any scene.** *Open and deliberately unasked, and it is forty-two days since the fourth refusal.*
- **A man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close**, who is the only person in a room who says *I do not know* out loud without being asked, and who was not in these twenty days. *Open.*
- **A man of about thirty-five whose right shoulder went on the thirty-first day of this flood, was reduced twice, cannot be set, did not go and was not made whole**, on a page in a stone as a debt with no line that will mend it, **and the page is now wrong and is going to stay wrong on purpose.** *Open, and unchanged.*
- **A woman of forty-one owed the work in her own words in the document that says the work is not written down anywhere.** *Open, and it is one of the twelve.*
- **A woman of about twenty-nine** is a different person from a boy of nine and from Curtis Okonkwo, twenty, of the second list, and was not in this batch and was not merged with anybody.
- **The six of the first group and the eleven of the second group** are still named, still with no land rights, no class, no standing and no workway, except one, and the one is not whole, has refused the asking once, and is owed a document that does not contain her.
- **A girl of sixteen** is one of the two people on no roll in this city, and has still not been asked about it. **The two people on no roll may never be witnesses, and a girl of seventeen who is on no roll is not one of them.**
- **Jonah Pike, 42, of the White Ledger**, was not in these twenty days and has said nothing since Chapter 0102. He is not convicted of anything and is not going to be, and the seat has not been offered a sixth time. **And Volume 04's plan opens on him.**
- **Wenna Cray's fifth statement of error, four sentences, all correct, stands unanswerable and unanswered and was not answered in this batch.**
- **Elsa Skell's figure and Elsa Skell's ninety-one acres, and the season of both, and a page in a stone that is wrong about her and cannot be corrected and will not be**, **Ilda Marsh's cart and a fourth refusal with nothing under it and now a page about it that does not name her village**, **the second list which did not go at all**, and **the four who read the join-line, who are still four.**

---

# Volume 04, Batch 0001 — Chapters 0151–0170, days 155 to 174

**Appended after the batch. These are the people as the twenty chapters left them. Nothing in this block is a plan.** The batch record is `state/batch-summaries/volume-04-batch-0001.md`.

## Adrian Vale — a man of thirty-two, on no roster of anything

**Volume 04's interior arc is paid and it is not a power and it is not growth.** He conceded the ninth sentence out loud on day 168 in front of about two hundred and forty people, in about four minutes, and it cost him, and nobody in the room improved on it and nobody apologised for him. He was offered a share with no post, no roll, no title, no wage, no conditions, no date and no way to revoke it, **and he did not refuse it in four seconds; it took four minutes, and he went looking for the place in it where somebody's reading is the price of somebody else's place, and there is not one.** He asked for four days to find out what it is. He asked one question in a hundred and sixty-six days, to a woman of about sixty-one with a barrow, and it was eleven words long, and she answered in one word that is not a noun, and he did not know what to do with it.

**He entered a delay, in his own hand, on day 164, in a gateway, in daylight: he is going to do nothing about the quarter for twenty-five days on purpose, and the delay is the length of the quarter exactly, and it is going to cost somebody a gate.** It is the first page he has written in this city that is not a refusal. He said *relief* out loud about the keeper of the Redroot gate's workway and entered it as a fault. He was told about Redroot Commons afterwards, at a gate, in nine seconds, and he was not in the room, and **not being in a room was a protection rather than a cost for the first time in a hundred and sixty-nine days, and he said one word, and the word was *good*.**

**On day 173 a man of forty-two gave him a name: *the witness*.** Not a title, not an office, not a post, not a form. He did not refuse it and it was not restraint and it was not agreement. **He said *say it again in a month*, and when a man of forty-four pointed out that he had said the same words to this man in a yard about a hundred days ago and then said *I will not*, he said: *I know, I said it to him and I did not mean it, and this time I do not know what I mean, and that is the difference and it is not a better answer.*** By nightfall the name was in four mouths and he was more findable than he had been that morning. **He holds no part of it. It is not a power, it is not a post, there is no column for it, and nobody can be written to for having said it.** He has no mark, no roster, no seat, no wage and no office, and he said so himself on day 154 in front of about two hundred and forty people, and a name that is a place a person can be found in does not improve that.

**He did not go to the Redroot gate on the twenty-fourth of the Long Month and entered that he would not, and he told the keeper of that gate in advance, at a door, in daylight, and did not improve on it.**

## Jonah Pike — a man of forty-two, warehouse supervisor in Leeds until the sixth day of this flood

**He is on a page for the first time since Chapter 0097 and the settlement is not a discovery: it has been on the page since Chapter 0087.** He walked nine miles in six hours of rain and put a printed sheet into a hand before he said a word, and he said out loud that the ground was taken in the seventh week without asking and that the paper money is worth nothing here and has never been worth anything. He answered the ninth sentence and eight of his nine sentences were the best arguments anybody had made in this city in a hundred and fifty-five days and the ninth one was not an argument. **He got the wage page right late and said so and asked for it to be entered.** He read the cost of a workway out in full, in daylight, with a date on it, and a man of about twenty-nine from his own workroom said the offer may be a breach of a term and that the only office that can answer is nine hundred miles away and the next time this city can put anything at all in front of it is about five weeks from the hearing on day 154.

**He offered Adrian a share with the post taken out and refused to read it out in a room, because the moment he reads it out it becomes a thing said in a room to about two hundred and forty people.** He gave Adrian the name *the witness* on day 173, in nine sentences, in daylight, in a room, and **he is right about eight sentences out of nine and the tenth part is always a local woman's word for a social fact, and the word in this volume is *order* and it is Renna Ord's and he arrived at it from the other end and neither of them has ever heard of the other.** He is not convicted of anything and is not going to be. **He does not improve on the ninth sentence and he does not take it back and he said that if he were Adrian he would write the page again tomorrow.**

## Hessa Ruun — a woman of about fifty, the water-count of the Reed Reach, who counts water on the fingers of her left hand

**She named the Long Month in a room on day 156 and named Redroot at a gate on day 157, once, in daylight, to the safest person in four hundred miles to say it to, and said why before she said it.** She said the fourth channel at Nine Elms is a gate and walked out onto the shut channel to prove it. **She said a gate is not owned and cannot be bought, that what a household has is the days on it, and a board, and a keeping of it, and a name in a speaker's record, and that the only way a household loses a gate is by agreeing, and that if it gives the days it still has the gate.** She wrote most of the Reach's list of persons and has been angry about nothing on it and would rather be angry about it than grateful about it. **She wrote the shut-days and the not-received figures on a board in chalk for the first time in nine years, in the rain, because a board is the only thing in this world a person can be handed.** She left word at a stall: nobody comes to my house, come to the gate. **About four of the nine people at that junction wanted to go and ask a woman of forty-eight in a fourth house a question and she wrote down that nobody did and that nobody went near the house.**

## The keeper of the Redroot gate — about thirty-eight, with a board in her left hand, who has kept a gate for nine years

**She is the cost of the batch and she is not a lesson and she is not rescued.** She held the gate board in her left hand through the whole of a kitchen argument on day 161 and did not put it down once, and the tell is nine years old. She read the cost of a workway out loud in full and said yes in nine sentences, and the reason is that **a duty to a place is the only wage anybody in this world has ever been able to offer her and nobody has ever read the cost out to her, not once, not in nine years, and she would have said yes in the fourth year and she is not going to pretend that is a reason to be glad.** She put the board down on a table and did not pick it up again. She works a rota she copied herself and has not told anybody she is doing it without the workway. **She chose the twenty-fourth of the Long Month, she is going to be asked for the days on the gate, and she has said in advance that she does not want Adrian in the room, and that she is going to be angry about it for a year, and that that is the whole of her. She has a boy of eight, a wage that is real, nine days of racks saved, and a gate, and in five days she is going to give away the days and keep the gate.** She will tell the boy on Thursday night herself and she asked for that to be entered.

## The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf — second handle: a slate she has never written on

**She is on the page in every one of the twenty chapters' orbit and she is the person who finds the reading of the day, three times.** She stood in the settlement's queue for two hours in the wet, carried nine sacks, took the first wage out of that store, and brought down nine miles the finding that **the man at the front knew her name and never asked her, and there is a list, and it is a good list, and it is a list.** She has been on nothing for a hundred and fifty-five days at the start of this batch and for a hundred and seventy days at the end of it. **She said Redroot's name back into the record when nobody wanted it on a page. She found the instrument in Chapter 0164: a share with no date on it is a share that goes on for as long as nobody objects, and it is not a lease, and nobody is going to be annoyed, because you pay. It is a queue.** She refused the naming of a commons once, on a correct principle, and she said *good* twice from about nine feet away at a door she did not go near. **The slate she has carried for nine years and has never written on is in her coat for both parts of day 159 and is not explained anywhere in this batch and is not going to be.**

## The man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board

**He has been refused by name four times in four years and stood on a quay since the ninth hour in case anything came, and what came was salt.** He walked all four miles of the causeway alone, said the answer in nine sentences in a room, and **did not name the gate and did not name the households and did not describe anybody, and the reason is his own: a description is a removal with a column for where the person is, and he has been on a board that goes down a river four times a year since he was thirty-one.** He got the workway's page shape from a stall-keeper in a conversation about a wage and neither of them thought of it as a lesson. He walked with the slow ones up the hill after day 166 and said one sentence and nobody wrote it down and he remembers it. **He is the man who told Adrian about Redroot Commons at a gate in nine seconds, and he said on the road afterwards that *good* is the only good word anybody in this city has said about a body that is not theirs, and that it is better than anything Adrian has made here, and he did not say it to his face.**

## A notary of fifty, a Lantern Concord notary who writes the public ledger entry

**She named the month in a room, in daylight, with a date on it, and gave nine sentences on what it costs, and said she was going to do it anyway and asked for that to be entered before and not after.** She is the only person in this city who keeps a count of days and had the date she was going to need before anybody came up the hill. **She did not grade her own paper and said so out loud, and a girl of seventeen wrote the number nine at the top of her slate and not the number of the sentences.** Somebody said *Sera* in the room and she did not answer. She has said no six times and the sixth was on day 154 and it was no to the opening of the box, **and the box is shut and stays shut.**

## A man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom — the one who is not Jonah Pike

**He has a hand that has been in three countries' counting houses and a way of standing at a board that makes it clear the board is his.** He read the cost of a workway out and read the fourth line wrong on purpose once, to see who would stop him, and put the chalk down and said *thank you* when about four people did. **He said out loud, in daylight, in front of about nine people, that the offer may be a breach of a term, and that the only office that can answer is nine hundred miles away, and that the next time this city can put anything at all in front of that office is about five weeks from the hearing on day 154 — so the question is real, correct, unanswered, and beside the point.** He came up the road to Redroot to hear how the settlement would be described and said he was not trying to argue. **He came down a line of thirty people at Redroot with a slate and could not tell which nine of the thirty were his, and did not ask, and went back up.** He was in a room with a door for five hours and worked out that a thing with no name can be walked over and a thing with a name has to be argued with, said both, said he did not know which one they wanted, and reported that he did not.

## A woman of twenty-four — the first public record in this city, on no roll of anything

**She wrote the count of this city's silences on a board on day 155 and it is sixty-one, one more than on day 154, and a day the wall says something on is not a silence however hard the day was.** She read the settlement's page before anybody spoke and put it face up on the step and said there was nothing in the eleven lines anybody could be obliged to do. She asked him, in a gateway, whether waiting was a plan or a place to stand. **She wrote a payment down when she had been asked not to and said why in her own words: so that in about four months nobody can say the payment was the only one nobody can find.** She copied nine pages of a better record by hand in six hours. **She has now entered, in her own words and without being asked, that not being in a room was a protection rather than a cost, and she called that the finding of the fifteenth day of the Long Month, and she does not know what any of it was for.**

## A girl of seventeen on no roll of anything

**She read Adrian's ninth sentence off her own slate on day 155 and it is seventy-eight words long and it is the longest single thing anybody has said in a yard in this city.** She put the payer's name at the top of every line and not the name of the person paid, and she counted the trader's nine sentences twice on her fingers and got nine both times, and she wrote the number nine at the top of her slate and not the sentences. **She refused, out loud, in a gateway, to write the word *commons* in her book — a name is a thing two people have said in one room, and a board on the flats is a thing anybody can walk up to — and she was right.** She wrote a date and the name *the witness* on a slate on day 173 and did not read it back and said why in about nine words: a name on a page is a place a person can be found in, and she has just watched one work, and she is not going to be the one who writes it down.

## Bel Dain — a woman of about fifty-nine who keeps a house and a boy of eight

**She holds a room with a door at Redroot that has a bench in it facing the door, and about four people noticed the bench inside about four minutes.** She refused the naming once, on a correct principle, and said why. **She did not count hands, because a count is a list, and said so.** She told the room the question that makes the naming dangerous and asked for it to be written on the wall in her own words: **a commons that can be asked is a danger and a commons that cannot be asked is a secret, and this city has spent a hundred and seventy days finding out that it cannot have either without paying somebody for it. She has a boy of eight and a gate and nine days of weather and she is not going to be the one who finds out by being asked.**

## Nerra Fenn — about thirty-eight, the keeper of the Nine Elms gate, who answers with her hands before she answers with her mouth

**She is not on the Reach's own list of people who may use the gate she keeps, and the reason is that nobody ever asked her, and she said it once and asked for it to be entered in her own words.** Nine years ago there was no board at that junction. **The day her name goes on that list the gate stops being hers and becomes the Reach's, and she has had a thing for nine years that nobody in four hundred miles can take off her, and she would rather have a gate that nobody has written down than a gate that is written down and belongs to everybody, and she has never been able to say whether that is a good answer.** Her name is chalked on a board at the junction with the date on it. **The rain took it. There is no second copy.**

## Elsa Skell — forty-four, of Corrow, one of the four who read the join-line, and she reads the edge

**She is now owed two correct figures about the same field and there is no instrument in four hundred or nine hundred miles for taking one off the other.** One is a page on the back of a stone nine miles up a road that a woman of about sixty-one with a barrow will not take down on purpose, and the other is a figure with a hand and a date put on the end of a line by a stranger who did not know the page existed. She took the money while he was still holding the chalk out, and she is not grateful and not angry, and she asked for one thing, which was that nobody write down that she took it. **The twenty-fifth reading is not taken and the measurement column is unchanged and no fifth reader is named.**

## Ivenn Marrow, Wenna Cray, Renna Ord, Roke, Priya Shah, Mina Okoye, Teodor Blaz, Perry Lask, Ottavia Reek, Renner Sallow, Dante Kuo, Ottoline Vask, Sabra Vint, Nora Vale, Hattie Park

**None of them is on a page in this batch except in a form already on a page before day 155, and Marrow is not mentioned once, and Wenna Cray's fifth statement of error is not answered, and the word *order* is Jonah's in Chapter 0169 and Renna Ord's and they are in the same book and not in the same room and no character in that chapter knows it.** Hettis Roke's eight and the eleven stones and the wooden case and the counter on a trestle in a book dated in Marrow's own hand are all where they were on day 154. **The trestle count is 114 on day 154 and is not this batch's and is not called by the name of the woman its owner's letter is addressed to.**

## The woman of forty-eight in a fourth house at Nine Elms

**Not asked, not approached, not named in any room where it could reach her, and nobody stood outside the fourth house.** She appears twice in this batch and both times as an entry about people who did not go near her — Chapter 0154, where about four of the nine at that junction wanted to and nobody did, and Chapter 0167, where the Reach's own rule is found to have a hole in it shaped exactly like her, **and the hole is not filled and the house is not approached.** The fourth channel stays shut for the whole batch. The volume is about a different gate and it does not spend her.

---

## Live after Volume 04, Batch 0002 — Chapters 0171 to 0190, days 175 to 194

**This block is the state at the Chapter 0190 boundary. The keeper of the Redroot gate, Jonah Pike, Adrian Vale, the woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by the wharf, Tamsin Quill, Hessa Ruun, Bel Dain, the girl of seventeen, the woman of twenty-four, the woman of twenty-nine, the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, the man of about forty-four who keeps a stall, the man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board, the man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom and the man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close are all in the twenty chapters just written. Nothing below is a plan.**

## Adrian Vale — Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing, and he does not change stage

**He did not break his twenty-five-day entry, and the entry expires at about the seventh hour of the evening of the thirty-fourth day of the Long Month, which is the day the quarter runs out.** He was not in the room on the twenty-fourth day of the Long Month and nobody thought of asking him whether the day suited him because he is not a party to it, and he found out afterwards from a girl of seventeen who stood in a yard and then walked eleven miles. **He asked three questions, in writing, four days beforehand, and did not ask her whether the day suited her, and wrote her answers on the back of his own arm at the near gate of the Lock of Salt.** He asked a question properly twice: of a woman of about thirty-six in a gateway, and of a body on the thirty-eighth day of the Long Month.

**What he holds is unchanged: no mark, no roster, no seat, no wage, no office, no title, no land right, no workway.** A share with the post taken out is still in a coat on the thirty-eighth day of the Long Month, twenty-five days after he was given four days to answer it, and he has said out loud that he gave up holding it as a refusal on the seventeenth day of the Long Month and is not going to give it up in a yard. **A name he did not choose, *the witness*, is in about four mouths and the wall has now put the name and the gate in one field, and the finding of the thirty-sixth day of the Long Month is that a person who is findable can be sent into any room and cannot be sent to a gate.**

**On the thirty-ninth day of the Long Month he stood at the back of a room for about four hours and did not answer a question that was put in his own words, in front of about nine people and about two hundred and forty who could hear, and that is the first thing he has done in a hundred and ninety-four days that he has not refused.** He performed no working, no threshold was opened, the other world is not named, and he offered nobody a workway and asked for none.

## The keeper of the Redroot gate — about thirty-eight, a board in her left hand, and the cost of Volume 04

**She said yes in one sentence in a kitchen on the twenty-fourth day of the Long Month, and about nine people were in the room, and there was no vote.** The cost was read out in full before the asking and she made the man read out the sentence he had shortened. **She said, in her own words, and did not soften it and nobody has improved on it: she chose the day, nobody has asked her anything in a hundred and seventy-nine days and that is not a complaint, and she is going to be angry about it for a year, and on the forty-eighth day of the Long Month she is going to be angry about it, and there is not going to be a second part of it and there is not going to be a reason.**

**She has not been rescued, has not been talked out of it, has not been given a reason to change her mind, has not said she is glad, and has not said she would say yes again, and no chapter may make her say it.** On the twenty-fifth day of the Long Month she worked the channel from the sixth hour to the fourth hour of the afternoon beside about nine people from a store she had said yes to the day before, and said not one word to any of them for about five hours, and said that the first wage is on a Tuesday and that it will make it worse and not better. On the twenty-sixth day she told a man of thirty-two he was sorry and he was not. On the twenty-seventh day she stood at the gate with the board in her left hand for the whole day while about nine hundred measures of her household's racks went up a causeway, and counted none of them, and said one sentence and it was a sentence the man said and not one she said. **She is the household's days and the finding of the thirty-ninth day of the Long Month is that the days are hers and only a household can give them back, and nobody went to get her.**

## Jonah Pike — forty-two, of the White Ledger, and a serious rival who is not a villain and is not defeated
