On the seventieth day of that month he stood under his own ninth line in a yard of about nine people and **refused to say whether the line works on a morning when there is nothing at the front of it, and has now refused it three times, in a market and in two yards.** On the seventy-fifth day of that month he came eleven miles to the top of a bank and gave the same answer in a third place and said he is not going to be a different one because somebody came up eleven miles to ask him in a better light. **He is a party to the compact, he has said the other half of his own sentence about a word and a wall, and he has still not mentioned the line in the trestle book, which is a different book, and nobody has asked him about it.**

- **THE KEEPER OF THE REDROOT GATE — FIFTY DAYS, AT THE TOP OF THAT CUT, SAYING NOTHING.** She is on a page once in these ten chapters, on the seventy-fourth day of the Long Month, at fifty days into a year of being angry, at the top of the cut with the board in her left hand, and she said nothing to anybody about it. She is not rescued, is not argued with, has not been given a reason to have done otherwise, has not said she is glad, has not said she would say it again, the phrase *the whole of her* is used about her zero times, no child was sent up that bank with a message, and nobody in that yard has said her name. **The year is not over and a date is not an ending.**

- **AND THE THINGS IN THE OTHER LISTS THAT DID NOT MOVE.** A woman of about fifty-six who keeps a shop four streets down from a market carried a wage nine miles on her arm for ten days and has nine measures under her counter hard since the thirty-ninth day of the Long Month that she paid a figure for on the twelfth day of that month. A man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close carried a wage four streets twice and did not open it, and asked how many sacks came out of that chest. A man of about twenty-seven who washes the store's board left yesterday's figures standing on a Tuesday morning for the first time since the seventh week of this flood. A man of about forty-four who keeps a stall said a length of wood like that comes off a rack and would not say whose. A man of about fifty-nine said in a market on the thirty-ninth day of the Long Month that a correct page nobody can check is the most dangerous sentence anybody has said in this flood, and is quoted about nine walks later by a man who washes a board. **A man of forty-two opened a chest on the fiftieth day of the Long Month in front of about nine people and on the seventieth came nine miles to stand at the back of his own queue with nothing at the front of it and did not go to the door.**

---

# Character State — Volume 05, Batch 0004 (Chapters 0231–0240, days 231 to 240)

**Compact live state for the next batch. These are the things the last ten days put on a page. Every prohibition standing at the end of Batch 0003 stands here unchanged, and this section does not relax one of them.**

## Adrian Vale — protagonist, Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing

- **In four of the ten chapters, and doing ordinary work in all four.** He carried a trough out from under a wall and emptied it in a yard about nine times before a queue was a queue on the seventy-seventh day of the Long Month; he stood in a back room and did not lift a sack that was standing in the middle of the floor on the seventy-ninth and said that a groove is not a hole and a sack in the middle of a floor is not a sack on a rota; he sat on a bank about nine feet from the keeper of the Redroot gate for four hours on the eighty-second, said nothing to her, said no name, and did not ask her anything; and he emptied a trough nine times in that yard on the eighty-fifth and did not go into a queue standing nine feet away from him.
- **A share with the post taken out** was given on the thirteenth day of the Long Month, day 168, and **NO FIGURE FOR IT IS ON A PAGE IN THESE TEN CHAPTERS.** He has taken it out of that coat once, on the seventy-first day of that month, and put it back in about the time it takes to turn a thing over, and he has said out loud that he does not know whether carrying it or carrying a trough is the thing he is better at. **A later writer may not put a number on it without a room, in daylight, with a date on it, and may not answer it.**
- **Not fetched once in ten days.** About nine people in this city can still fetch him by a name he did not choose and about four of the four who waited for somebody to say a name at the top of that cut on the eighty-second did not say it. **A refusal to fetch is not an unfetchability and about four people in this city know the difference and he is one of them.**
- **No chapter offered him a workway and he asked for none. No chapter turned the name he did not choose into a qualification. Nobody wrote him a column. No chapter asked him about a gate. He performed no working, no threshold was opened, the other world is not named once in the ten chapters, and nobody stood outside the fourth house at Nine Elms.**

## The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall and who wrote the ninth line

- **Came up nine miles on the eighty-fourth day of the Long Month with a piece of chalk in his right hand and did not put the date back at the top of the page, and put the chalk in his pocket.** He said that what is left of that page is nine lines in two hands with a date gone off the top of it, and that a page with a date on it is a page about a day while a page with a day on it is a page anybody can be angry at, and that both of those were true for thirty-eight days and only one of them is left.
- **He has still not said whether the ninth line works, and nobody asked him in the last ten days.** A later writer may not resolve it, may not move the ninth line, and may not have him put the date back.
- **On the seventy-seventh day of that month he stood in that yard at the ninth hour and read the ninth line without looking up for the whole of a Tuesday and did not say whether it works, and he read his own line from about nine feet under it on the eighty-fourth without moving his head.**

## The man of about twenty-seven who washes the store's board

- **Has not written on that board since the seventieth day of the Long Month and the figures on it are the figures of the sixty-ninth day of that month, which is sixteen days old at the eighty-fifth.** He has washed it every morning and hung it back up every morning.
- **Put the board back up on the wall on the seventy-sixth day of that month after the wind took it off and about nine feet, and did not write on it, and gave the reason: on the seventieth he decided, and this morning the board came off the wall, and one of those is a person doing a thing and the other is a thing happening to a board.**
- **Said out loud on the seventy-ninth that the board is the day and the posting is the page, and that a day is a thing that happens once and a page is a thing that stays up, and that he only worked that out when the wind took the day off the wall.** He also said on the eighty-fifth that he is not going to be the man who puts a bag on a page, and that a groove is not a column and a crust is not a figure.
- **About nine people read that board and about four read the posting, and they are about a foot apart and have been since the forty-sixth day of the Long Month, and the wind takes them at different rates.**

## The woman of about thirty-four in the store on the upper road

- **Swept the floor of the back room for the first time since the fifty-sixth day of that month, on the seventy-ninth, and nobody asked her to, and she asked for that to be said before anything else about the floor.** She is on no list in that building and she is paid out of a chest that has not been opened since the fifty-sixth day of that month.
- **Has not opened the chest. Did not open it on either Tuesday in the last ten days and said again that she is not a person who gets to say which.**
- **Did not pick up the bag of salt a man put down on the boards of her yard on the eighty-fifth, and said she has not got anybody in that yard who can say whether a wage is a thing a person is given or a thing a person has, and that about four hundred and forty people on eleven miles of flats have neither.**
- **The four sacks with a knot that is not the knot on the other eight are still four sacks, are still at the bottom of what she put them at the bottom of on the fifty-sixth day of that month, have still never been weighed, and she says she is not going to find out which sack a crust on the floor came out of from a floor.**

## The woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road

- **Has still not chosen a day to ask in and has not since the fifty-sixth day of the Long Month.** She was at the top of the cut on the Reach on the seventy-eighth and at the top of the Redroot cut on the eighty-second, and she asked for nothing on either morning.
- **Said on the seventy-eighth that she comes up that road on days that are not Tuesdays because a Tuesday is a day about four hundred and forty people are somewhere else on, and that she would like that written down and not written down, in that order, and has not decided which.**
- **Saw the keeper turn her board face down against her leg and said she was not going to say a word about it and would like it noticed that she did not say a word about it.** A later writer may not have her ask in a yard and may not have her choose a day in a yard.

## The woman of about thirty-four who keeps a stall by a wharf

- **Did not touch a wage a man of about thirty-one put on the boards of her stall on the eighty-first day of the Long Month, and left a gap about the width of a person between herself and it.** She has been on a page since the forty-fifth day of that month and it is thirty-six days, and about four people in that market know what that is and about nine of them do not, and she asked in advance not to be thanked and has not been. **Nobody in the ten chapters said her name.**

## The man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board

- **Carried the same bag against his chest from the sixty-second day of the Long Month to the eighty-fifth, which is twenty-three days, and put it down on the boards of the yard of the store on the upper road on the eighty-fifth and did not open it and did not say what was in it.** Nobody in that yard picked it up.
- **Said out loud that he put it down and has not decided he is carrying it, and that a man who is carrying a thing in front of himself cannot be told anything by anybody standing behind him, and that the mark the bag left on the front of his coat is still on the coat.**
- **Break is not open. The salt went hard on the sixty-sixth day of that month and a bar will not go into it and a later writer may not have anybody break it in a room, and may not say what a wage in salt is worth.**

## The man of about fifty-two who digs

- **The hole nine feet from the one four people measure has filled in with the dust off eleven miles of flats and is not a hole, and a rod in it has come up about four inches in its standing since the fifty-ninth day of that month.** He came up the bank on the seventy-eighth and said the hole finished itself and that he is not going to put a rod in a third one for a man who wants a figure.
- **Has still not said what the one thing was that the second hole was for, and has said out loud that he has not said it because he has not found it out and has not said it because of what it is, and neither.**
- **The beam with one pan on it has stood in that yard since the seventy-third day of that month with a sack in the pan. He has not carried it home and has not replaced the pan and will not.** He came and looked at it on the eighty-first and said that everything in that yard had happened without him in it for twelve days.
- **He has not said what day the water comes back and a later writer may not make him say it.** The channel is still dry and the four weeks is still a direction and still unresolved.

## The keeper of the Redroot gate

- **Fifty-eight days into a year of being angry on the eighty-second day of the Long Month, and the year is not over.** She was at the top of the cut from first light and had not put the board down once.
- **At about the ninth hour she took her left hand off the board, turned it face down against her leg, and put her hand back on the outside of it, and did not stop working the channel, and said no word to anybody about it.** The board is not on a list of anything, it carries two dates in two hands and a set of knife marks, and the twenty-fourth is not on it, and she has not had it out of her hand since the forty-eighth day of the Long Month.
- **She said out loud that there is not going to be a second page going face down from her, and that a year is a year and it has not run out, and she said the last three sentences of that to the boards of the bank and not to anybody.** About four people at the top of that cut heard all three and about nine of them heard nothing at all.
- **She is not rescued, not argued with, not given a reason to have done otherwise, has not said she is glad, has not said she would say it again, the phrase *the whole of her* is used about her zero times, no child was sent up that bank with a message, and nobody at the top of that cut said her name.** Adrian Vale sat about nine feet from her for four hours and did not say a word to her and did not ask her anything. **A later writer may not attach a weekday to the twenty-fourth day of the Long Month while the conflict at `bible/power-system.md` §27.14 stands.**

## The man of about thirty-two who stands on the bank

- **Went down the bank of the cut on the Reach on the eighty-third day of the Long Month, which is the seventh Monday in a row he has stood on that bank, and had not come down it since before the fifty-ninth day of that month.** The six Mondays before that one are the forty-first, the forty-eighth, the fifty-fifth, the sixty-second, the sixty-ninth and the seventy-sixth days of the Long Month, and a reader can check the chain.
- **He has no mark, no workway, no title and no seat and is on no roster of anything, and he is not Adrian Vale, and no chapter of this batch has the two of them in it.**
- **He did not come up the bank to a woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder and he did not ask her anything, and he touched none of the three things at the bottom of the cut.** A later writer may not have him ask, and may not have him be fetched.

## The woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder

- **Did not clear a hole out with her hand on the seventy-eighth day of that month and gave the reason out loud: that if she clears it this morning then it is a hole a man put his hand in, and it would be the beginning of one by a woman who cannot get a rack above her shoulder, which is a sentence she has not got a use for.** She has a refusal with a date on it that she has not shown anybody and she has not asked anybody anything.
- **Came down the bank at the bottom of the Redroot cut on the eightieth day of that month after standing at the top of it for an hour, and said that about four people in this city have worked out that she has not come down that bank in twenty-one days and about nine of them have worked out that she has not come down it because of what is at the top of it.**
- **The man of about fifty-two who digs did not ask her to get a hand under a rack, and said out loud that he had said no about wood three times in that cut and that a man who has said no three times about a thing has to be careful about who he asks a fourth time.**

## The woman of about fifty who counts water

- **Would not take a figure off the second hole on the seventy-eighth day of the Long Month, and would not take one off a channel that has been dry for forty-one days on the eighty-second, and said the reason out loud both times: a figure is a thing she takes and there is no figure in a heap of dust and a figure off a dry channel would be a figure about a hole, and a hole that four people measure is a column about a hole, and she said that out loud twice and would not say it a third time on a Sunday.**
- **About four people in this city would have a figure off that cut by the second hour of the afternoon and about nine of them would have it by the seventh hour of the evening in a town nine hundred miles from here.** A later writer may not take a reading for her.

## The man of about thirty-one with a hand that will not close

- **Put a wage he has had for twenty-five days on the boards of a stall by a wharf on the eighty-first day of the Long Month, carried it four streets twice on the sixty-eighth day of that month, and has not opened it. At about the sixth hour of the afternoon his hand opened, on its own, and stayed open for about as long as it takes to take a board off a stack, and shut again and did not come open a second time that day.** Nobody in that market said a word about it. He put the hand under the boards where nobody could see it.
- **He says he will want to say something about it in about four days and will not, and that four people in that market are going to write a word for a hand that opens once and nine of them are not, and he is not going to have his first.**
- **He has not got a house with a floor he would put that wage on and he has got a coat with a mark on the front of it, and that is the whole of what he has got.**

## The girl of seventeen

- **Stood at the rail of that wharf from the fourth hour of the afternoon on the eighty-first day of the Long Month with a plain slate under her arm and was not asked anything at all, and read the top of it out under her breath and stopped in the middle of a line and put the slate face down on the rail.** She is the only person in that market who is not on the front of any page in this city, and her own slate has her name at the top of it and that is the only reason she is standing there.
- **She said that a girl of seventeen is not a person anybody comes to with a question, and that about four people in this city have worked out that being unfindable cost her nothing and about nine of them have not, and that she has worked out this cannot go on the way it has been going and she is not going to be the one who writes down the day it stops.** Being unfindable is not a qualification and a later writer may not make it one, may not have anybody ask her whether she is all right as a way of ending a chapter, and may not resolve her.

## The woman of about fifty-nine who keeps the house at the bottom of the Redroot cut

- **Said out loud on the eightieth day of the Long Month that she has gone from a stripe on four boards to four inches of a corner and is not going to say what the four inches are about, and that she wants four inches of a corner on her boards for about nine years and nothing else.** The stripe was there from the sixty-first day of that month and the boards went the colour of the boards under them on the seventy-fourth and the stripe went with them, and nobody scrubbed it out.
- **She said again that nobody in that yard is going to count the sacks on her step and that a count of sacks is a count of what the days bought and the day somebody counts that is the day it is a price, and it is not a price, it is a sack on a step.** She has not asked the man of about forty who carries sacks where they come from, and has not asked him in the last ten days either. **A later writer may not count them and may not ask.**

## The boy of eight

- **Unties a length of rope off a wall in that yard and ties it again, with his teeth in the end of it, every morning since the seventy-fourth day of the Long Month, and his hands went white at the rope about halfway this morning.** The rope is about nine years old and it is not tied to anything and about nine people in this city know that and about four of them have said it out loud and nobody has said it in that yard.
- **His step and that rope are nine feet apart and the woman of about fifty-nine who keeps that house said in that yard that nine feet is the right distance and that she is not going to change it.** A later writer may not send him up a bank with a message.

## The man of about forty-two

- **Stood at the back of a queue for the fifth and sixth Tuesdays in the last ten days with his hands in his pockets and did not go to the front of it and did not go into the store.** He has had his hands in his pockets since the forty-fifth day of the Long Month at the back of a line of his own making.
- **On the seventy-seventh day of that month he said he has been in this flood for two hundred and thirty-one days and has five of those Tuesdays and nothing has come out of that chest since the fifty-sixth day of it, and that a board which has not been written on since the seventieth day of that month is more use to about four hundred and forty people on eleven miles of flats than anything he has ever said, and that he has not got a figure for that and is not going to put one on it.**
- **He is not a villain, he did not leave, and he is not going to say the second thing the yard is waiting for.** A later writer may not give him a plan and may not have him turn anybody out.

## The woman of about twenty-four who is on no roll of anything

- **Put a slate down on the boards of the yard of the store on the upper road at about the ninth hour on the eighty-fourth day of the Long Month and asked Shall I write this down, and nobody in that yard answered her.** It is the third time in about three weeks that she has asked it in a yard, on the sixty-sixth day of the Long Month and again on the seventy-third, and **about four people in this city have worked out that nobody in this city has ever not answered it, and about nine of them have worked out that this morning somebody did not answer it.** She picked the slate up and put it back under her arm.
- **She has carried that slate since the forty-sixth day of the Long Month and has never once been told to put it down, and she has written down every decision this city has made since the second day of this flood, and a decision on a slate is a page and she knows what a page is.**

## The man of about twenty-nine from the settlement's workroom

- **Will not put his slate face up a second time, and gave the reason out loud on the seventy-seventh day of the Long Month: a thing anybody can walk up to and look at a second time is a thing about the person who looked and not about the thing, and he would rather be a man whose slate nobody in this city can check.** He changed which side of himself it was on and put it under the other arm.
- **He is not in that yard, and the man of about thirty-four who wrote the ninth line said out loud on the eighty-fourth that eight of the nine lines on that page are in his hand and the ninth is the man of about thirty-four's.**
- **His decision on the slate from the seventieth day of the Long Month stands and has not been withdrawn, and about nine people in this city can still fetch Adrian Vale by a name he did not choose and a decision not to fetch is not an unfetchability.**

## The man of about forty-four who keeps a stall

- **Came up the nine miles on the seventy-ninth day of the Long Month with a basket he did not need to bring and stood in a doorway and did not open a twelfth entry in his column of costs.** He has eleven entries and the one that is paid is the thirteenth day of the new month, day 168, and there is no figure at the end of any of them.
- **He said a sweep could be put against a sack and against a place at a fire and against a day of the queue, and that there is no figure at the end of any of those three sentences, and that he has been trying to write one since the forty-fourth day of the Long Month and has got about nine pages of a slate with nothing on the end of the lines, and that he has come up this road four times to tell about four people in that room that he has got nothing to put on the end of one.**
- **He is a party to the compact and a cost is what a person says they are paying and a price is what a person says it is worth, and a later writer may not recite the column and may not open a twelfth entry.**

## The woman of about fifty-six who keeps a shop four streets down from a market

- **Has had nine measures under her counter hard since the thirty-ninth day of the Long Month, paid a figure for them on the twelfth day of that month, and has not opened them. She moved them off a shelf against an outside wall on the seventy-sixth day of that month and would not say where they are, and the reason is the one about a shelf and the wind and not the one about the shelf.**
- **Walked nine miles to the yard of the store on the upper road on the seventy-sixth, the seventy-seventh and the eighty-fourth days of that month and did not go into the yard on any of the three.** She stood at the gate on the last one and said a shopkeeper is the only person in four hundred miles whose whole trade is a thing that happened somewhere else, and that she cares about what she saw at that wharf more than anything in her shop and cannot put it on a shelf without it being a price.
- **She has never said one word to the man of about thirty-one about a wage, and she has known that they are four streets apart and carrying a thing they have not opened since the fifty-sixth day of that month, and she has not gone four streets and is not going to.**

## The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register

- **Came along the boards of that market at about the fifth hour of the afternoon on the eighty-first day of the Long Month with a page in her own pocket with a date on the back of it, and did not take it out.** It is not a slate and it is not in a book and it has two names on the front of it and no third.
- **She would not put a date on the back of that page, and gave the reason: a woman standing nine feet from her had asked what a date was in four words at the seventh hour of the evening on the thirtieth day of the Long Month and was told nothing, and she is not going to be the second person in this city to hand a date to a person who is not asking for one.**
- **The delegation of four people and a question has not left, nobody has been asked whether they will go, a figure of seventy-two days is on it with no owner, and no figure in the last ten chapters is on it. *To open nothing* is her sentence and she will not say it a fourth time and nobody may say it for her, and it is in the prose of none of these ten chapters.**

## Marrow, the keeper of the Redroot gate's year, the trestle man and the others

- **Ivenn Marrow: 0 mentions in these ten chapters and no chapter of this batch mentions the room over a yard, the book on the trestle or the line in it. He is neither convicted nor made useless, nobody asked him about anything, and the trestle count and the line in that book that is not a number are on no page in these ten days.**
- **The man who carries sacks did not come up the bank at the bottom of the Redroot cut in the last ten days and nobody in that yard said a word about that to anybody. A later writer may not ask and may not say whether the days still buy a sack.**
- **The man who has carried racks up and down that cut since the second month of this flood put a hand on a rack on the eighty-third day of the Long Month and took it off again and did not give the reason he has been holding since the seventy-second day of that month. The rack is still at the edge of the bottom of that cut with the rope coiled on the top length, and a later writer may not have him put it in and may not have him give the reason in a yard with about nine people in it.**
- **The thirty-nine men under the near gate of the Lock of Salt are on no line of the posting and no figure for what they are owed is on a page in these last forty days.**

---

## Volume 05, Batch 0005 — the authoritative block for the next Volume 05 phase

**Append-only. The block above is Batch 0004's and is superseded by this one. Where this file and a chapter disagree about a day, a name or a figure, the chapter is right. Batch record: `state/batch-summaries/volume-05-batch-0005.md`. Days 241 to 250 of the flood, the eighty-sixth to the ninety-fifth day of the Long Month.**

**Adrian Vale — 32, protagonist. Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing, and nothing in this batch moved that.** In five of the ten chapters (0241, 0242, 0244, 0246, 0249) and did ordinary work in four of them. He carried a full trough into a yard and went round a sack to bring it the last nine feet without looking at his own feet (the eighty-sixth). He came down a bank eleven miles to a rope that was not his and did not offer to lift it, and said out loud why — that nobody has ever handed him a rope and asked him to carry it up a bank, and the second he does that he is a man who is available, and availability is the only thing he has got (the eighty-seventh). He stood at a gate with nothing to carry and came anyway, and said out loud that he has run out of reasons and has not admitted it (the eighty-ninth). He stood in the pocket between a bent queue-line and a wall, which is the only place in that yard where a person can stand and not be in anything (the ninety-first). **And on the ninety-fourth he came eleven miles to the top of the Redroot cut to ask the keeper of the Redroot gate a question and did not ask it, and told her so at the ninth hour in daylight in front of about four people, and the wall said one line to him and the line was a warning about his own limit and not an answer, and he shut his hand and put it behind him and said nothing about where the words had come from.** He was not fetched once. Nobody offered him anything and he asked for nothing. No chapter asked him about a gate and no chapter turned the name he did not choose into a qualification. **A share with the post taken out was given on the thirteenth day of the Long Month, day 168, and NO FIGURE FOR IT IS ON A PAGE IN CHAPTERS 0241 TO 0250; the only figure in a mouth for it in this manuscript is fifty-eight, twice, at `chapter-0226.md`, and it is unanswered.** He performed no working, no threshold was opened, and the other world is not named once in the ten chapters. **What he has learned in these ten days, in his own words: that a man who comes up a road nine miles with nothing has run out of reasons before he has run out of things to do; and that a man who says a word about a man not fetching him has asked to be fetched.**

**The keeper of the Redroot gate — 38.** In two chapters (0247, 0249) and in both of them the finding is about a broom and not about her. She worked the invert of a channel that has had no water in it since the forty-first day of the Long Month, with a stick, from the first hour to the eleventh, on a day when there was nothing in the channel to work, and she keeps a count of it on a board that is on no list. A broom was put down about nine feet from her on the ninety-second and she looked at it once and did not look at it again, and she did not pick it up, and she did not say thank you and she did not say no. On the ninety-fourth there were two brooms and she said nobody brought the second one and that a second thing in a place is not an answer. **She is seventy-one days into a year of being angry that has not run out. She was rescued in no chapter, argued with in no chapter, given a reason in no chapter, made glad in no chapter; she said nothing about the twenty-fourth, about her year, or about the days; a child was sent nowhere; *the whole of her* is at zero; a second page did not go face down from her; and no date was put on her board.**

**The woman of about thirty-four in that store, on no list in it.** In seven of the ten chapters, the connective tissue of the batch. She went round a sack four times in a quarter of an hour and was the first person in this city to describe what she was doing. She refused to put a boot on the nine feet of ground the sack is standing on, and gave the reason: the second she puts a boot on it, it is not that any more. She has eleven sacks along a wall, four of them with a knot that is not the knot on the other eight, a groove four inches deep at the end they go down at with a crust of salt in the bottom of it, and she has said out loud that she put those four at the bottom so that she could see that she had not opened them. She asked the man of about thirty-four whether the ninth line works and was refused. She said out loud that she would rather a crust in a groove stayed in a groove than got a figure on it, and that she would rather this yard had nothing anybody could check than one thing. **She is on no list in that building and a later writer may not put her on one.**

**The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall and wrote the ninth line.** In three chapters (0244, 0246, 0250). Came up the road on the eighty-ninth with a piece of chalk in his coat from the eighty-fourth and did not use it; refused for the fourth time and gave a new reason, and refused for the fifth time on the ninety-first and said the reason has not changed. **He has now refused five times in this manuscript, four of them on a page, and the new reason is his and may not be improved on: a line is not tested by the man who wrote it.** He is never in a sentence with any of the four women of about thirty-four and this is now a check, not a habit.

**The man of about twenty-seven who washes that store's board.** In seven of the ten. Washed the board every morning since the seventh week of this flood and did not write on it since the seventieth day of the Long Month, whose figures are for the sixty-ninth. **On the eighty-ninth he stopped throwing water at the wall above the board and has not resumed, and on the ninety-fifth he said out loud that he had decided not to and that it was the first decision he had made about that wall since the seventy-sixth.** He said out loud that he can read the ninth line from the foot of that wall and has not said one word about what it says. He carried a sack of salt for a quarter of an hour in this batch and carried it nine miles in Batch 0002 and he is not the man who made that bag.

**The man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board.** In three chapters (0246, 0248, 0250). Carried a quarter of a sack against his chest from the sixty-second day of the Long Month and put it on the boards of that yard on the eighty-fifth, and it is an island of nine feet of ground nobody walks on, and he has not picked it up and has not broken it and says he does not know what it is for and that it is the fourth time in ten days he has said that to somebody. He came up that road three times between the eighty-fifth and the ninety-fifth and on the last morning stood at the gate and looked at the ground instead of at the bag and said out loud that the day it is dated from is the day somebody decided to leave it, and that he did not decide to leave it and cannot make anybody in that yard agree that it is his.

**The man of about thirty-two who stands on the bank. NOT ADRIAN VALE AND NEVER IN A CHAPTER WITH HIM.** In one chapter (0245) and Adrian is not in it. Came down the bank of the Redroot cut on the ninetieth day of the Long Month, which is the eighth Monday in a row he has stood on it, for the second time he has ever come down it; the last time was the eighty-third and the time before that was before the fifty-ninth. He lifted the coil at four points, carried it up and put it on the top length of a rack that is not his, tied it wrong, and said out loud that he did not know how he tied it. He went past a rod he put in a crack on the sixty-ninth day of that month twice and did not touch it. **At the tenth hour he said to nobody that he was finished and not stopped, that he was not coming back, and that he did not want to say it out loud before the rope was back. He still has no mark, no workway, no title and no seat, and he still cannot go on the delegation, and nobody in these ten days asked him anything.**

**The woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder.** In two chapters (0242, 0245). Came down a bank after a coil of rope that was not hers, got her fingers under it a hand's width at a time, rolled it on its side and pushed it four inches at a time until it lay the way rope lies when nobody has opened it, and refused to carry it up. **She told a stranger on the eighty-seventh day of that month that she can carry a length of wood and cannot carry a rack and that there is a reason and she is not going to say it, and that nobody has asked her the difference and she would like it kept that way. She has still not shown anybody her refusal and a later writer may not have her asked about it in a yard.**

**The girl of seventeen.** In two chapters (0243, 0246). Sweeps the boards of a stall by a wharf every market evening and will not move a wage to sweep under it, because a broom going over a wage is a thing a person watches. Did not take a man's arm when he put his foot in a gap four feet from her. **Said out loud on the ninety-first that a girl of seventeen is not a person a market comes to with a hand out, that a woman offered her a share out of her own stall on the sixty-eighth and she said no, and that the day she takes a share is the day there is a person in four hundred miles who can find out whether she is all right. Being unfindable is not a qualification and a chapter may not resolve her.**

**The man of about fifty-two who digs.** In two chapters (0242, 0248). Said out loud again that he is not going to put a rod in a third hole and that a man who stops measuring and says so out loud has found the end of the thing he was measuring. **Put his own coat over the one pan on the ninety-third and did not turn the pan out and gave the reason out loud: a pan in the open has the day on the top of it, and weighing what is under the day is measuring a yard instead of a sack. He has not been in that yard in twenty days and everything in it has happened without him in it.** The pan is not replaced, the beam is not carried home, and the coat is not taken off in a chapter.

**The man of about fifty-six who keeps a shop four streets down from a market.** In one chapter (0243), at the end of it, and she said one thing at that stall that about four people in the row heard and nobody repeated. Her nine measures have been hard under her counter since the thirty-ninth day of the Long Month and she has not opened them.

**The man of about forty-two.** In one chapter (0246), at the back of a line that is not the one he made, and he said that the man at the back of a line is a man with nothing to decide and that this morning he is a man watching two lines that are not his.

**The man of about thirty-one with the hand that will not close.** **IN NO CHAPTER OF BATCH 0005.** He is on the page in `chapter-0236.md`, where a hand of his opened once on its own on the eighty-first day of the Long Month on the boards of a stall by a wharf, and nobody in that market said a word about it, and he is the man who will not have the first word written for it. **A first draft of `chapter-0250.md` had him going through the Redroot gate with his hand shut and had the woman of about thirty-four in the store nine miles away reporting three questions about that hand in fourteen days; the review repair pass took both out, because the hand belongs to the stall the woman of about thirty-four by the wharf keeps and the store woman is a different person, and the descriptor map at the batch record's §10.8 is right and the prose was wrong.** **No figure for that hand is on a page in Chapters 0241 to 0250 and none may be attributed to this batch. A hand that opens once is still not a thing anybody in this city has a word for, and no chapter of this batch made it a symptom and none made it a cure.**

**The man of about thirty-four from the workroom, the man of about twenty-nine from the workroom, the man of about forty-four with eleven entries in a column, the man of about twenty-four who is on no roll of anything, the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register, the man of about fifty who counts water, the woman of about fifty-nine who keeps that house, the woman of about thirty-four from a fourth household on the road.** Present in Chapters 0247 and 0249 respectively for the fourth household, in Chapters 0241 and 0246 for the two workroom men, and **not present at all** in the other five. Nobody in those five chapters wrote a twelfth entry in a cost column, dated a page in a market, put a figure on the trestle, said *to open nothing*, asked whether the four will go, or counted a sack.

**Ivenn Marrow is in no chapter of Batch 0005 and nobody asked him about anything.** He is neither convicted nor made useless, and he has still not mentioned the line in his book, and the trestle count is on no page in these fifty days.

---

## Volume 06, Batch 0001 — Chapters 0251 to 0260, days 251 to 260. APPEND-ONLY. The block above is Volume 05 Batch 0005's and nothing in it is superseded. Where this file and a chapter disagree about a day, a name or a figure, the chapter is right. Batch record: `state/batch-summaries/volume-06-batch-0001.md`, and §14 of it is the review repair pass, and this block has the repair's rulings in it.

**Adrian Vale — 32, protagonist. Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing, and nothing in this batch moved that.** On five of the ten pages (0251, 0252, 0254, 0259, 0260) and did ordinary work in all of them. Fetched once, on day 251, by a woman of about twenty-four **who was not sent by anybody**, and he answered to a name he did not choose in about a second and entered that he had answered quickly. He wrote nine words on a slate in Chapter 0252 and did not read them out and let the register-keeper read them in the room at the other end of the hall. He noticed a man turn a thing over twice in Chapter 0254 and entered that he had noticed. He read an entry twice in Chapter 0259 and said nothing and nobody asked him. **And in Chapter 0260 he walked the whole way up the road past this city with nothing in his hands, gave the four conditions of the instrument to a person in her own hand, and walked the whole way back with nothing in his hands, and the distance is on no page in this manuscript because the Marches are at no distance.** He was not fetched again in the batch and nobody asked him whether a day suited him. He performed no working, no threshold was opened, the Narrow Mark was not opened, the other world is not named once in the ten chapters, nobody offered him a workway and he asked for none, no chapter asked him a question about a gate, and his passage privilege is not spent, not threatened and not named. **What he has learned in these ten days, in his own words: that an instrument which has never once been kind to the man who built it has still never been wrong about anything, and that he has not worked out how those two are supposed to fit together.**

**Hettis Roke — 44, of the Hearthguard of the outer ward, nineteen years in, keeper of the ground behind the ordinary wall.** In two chapters (0254, 0259) and both are him refusing or failing to answer, and he is neither a discovery nor a threat. He turns a waxed slate over and turns it back; **his second handle is that slate and the turns are not thinking, he says, he was checking that the other side was still there.** In 0254 he could not say yes and could not say no and asked for that to be entered first, and he gave the five figures — **eight, every year, nineteen years, four times a year, nobody** — in a voice with nothing in it, and the register-keeper wrote all five on a second slate and **did not join them up**, and a girl of seventeen copied them onto a third slate in her own hand and put no mark beside any of them. **In 0259 he refused to carry the case west before the sentence was finished, his reason is nine words and is the whole of it, and then he did the one thing in that chapter that is his and is not a refusal: he asked, in writing and four days ahead, for the case to be read to the three villages by somebody who is not carrying it, and nobody in four hundred miles has ever asked for that.** He said no four times in this city and has never said yes to anything. He asked once, a long time ago, to be asked again on a different day, and did not ask today, and nobody in the room was going to be the one to ask him. **His eight were asked about for the first time since day 137, on a day he did not choose, and he has not been asked about them again since.**

**The woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register.** In five chapters (0251, 0252 at the other end of the hall, 0254, 0257, 0258, 0259). **Her second handle is a second slate she writes on while somebody else reads the first, and she will not join two figures up**: *a count in a column is one thing and a count under a date is another, and if I join those five figures together on a slate in this room then somebody will come along in about four months with a book and find a column with my hand at the bottom of it.* She is the account of the document and she says so and refuses to translate. **She has refused three things out loud in two hundred and fifty-eight days and named the cost before saying it every time, and Chapter 0258 is the first time she was told she was right and it did not help and the chapter says so.** **Tamsin Quill and she are two people and nothing in this batch settles whether they are one woman; they were in the same building on the same day in two rooms, and the two slates were carried between them by the man of about thirty-nine, and no chapter of this batch puts them in one sentence or in one room.**

**The man of forty-one in a very good coat.** In four chapters (0251, 0252, 0254, 0257, 0259) and he is not a villain. **His second handle is the inside of a coat, which he checks with his hand before he answers, twice, and nobody in the room remarks on it.** He has been in the room at the top of that road nine hundred miles away and he is the only person in four hundred miles who cannot be checked by anybody in the room he is standing in. **He is the mouth of Regent Solenne Dary's nine sentences and not her own, and he will not give the figure on her return and says why, and he is the only one of them who can say what she did not say and what she did not say is that the days on a gate are the Crown's to schedule, because nobody asked her.** He has never said how he knows what is on a sheet and was not going to, and that not-saying was a hand going into a coat and coming out again and nothing else. **He left a hand inside his coat for a whole hour in Chapter 0259 and did not take it out once.**

**Sera Vail — notary of fifty, twenty-six years a notary, and the notary of about nine hundred things and about nine hundred of them were somebody else's sentence.** In one chapter (0256) and she carries the fifth form and the volume's one panel. **Her second handle is a date she has to write.** She could not certify a day and said the cost out loud before she named anything, **that a name invented by a notary is a name an office can use, and in about four months a clerk nine hundred miles away is going to write it at the head of a page and nobody in that building will be able to tell whether it means what she means**, and that she has been the only person in this city keeping a count of her own counting since the fortieth day of this flood and is tired of it. **She named the Short Month, sixty days, ending on its sixtieth day, on day 256, which is also the hundred and first day of the Long Month by the form that ended the day before, and both dates on that one table are correct and only one of them was chosen by a person.** She refused the wall's line out loud in about four seconds and **said what refusing it cost: that she has just invented a name in a room and she is going to keep the name.** She has put her own name on a month twice in a hundred days, the first time because there was no page at all and the second because a page had a column in it.

**Jonah Pike — 42, warehouse supervisor on a river in another country, right about nine parts in ten, on a page in Volume 06 in Chapters 0253 and 0258 and at zero in the other eight.** He is not a discovery and he is not a villain and this volume does not defeat him and he does not leave. **His second handle is a printed sheet with a wage on it that he hands over before he says a word, and the chapter says it is the same tell as in Chapter 0151 and does not explain it.** In 0253 he said three things about the docket and was right about two and refused to guess at the third in a yard. He put his copy of the docket down on the boards of a stall rather than carry it. **In 0258 he counted nine people in a yard, one through nine, out loud, said a count is a number and a list is a roll, and the count of who said what is a list, and he can pay the wage out of a pocket and cannot post it and both are true on the same morning and a favour is a thing that stops.**

**Alys Kern — keeper of a gate on the road west, named out loud in a room in daylight with a date on it in Chapter 0257 by the man of forty-one in a very good coat.** Nobody has said her name to her and nobody has been sent. The stretch of road she keeps is called the Hearth Marches, which is the only name on any of the five Crown sheets that is the name of a place and not the name of an office. **Her second handle is a key on a string over her shoulder in the front of her apron, and she does not turn it over once, and it is the same in every chapter she is in — and that is the whole of what is known about her and she is the person the question is going to be put to.** **In Chapter 0260 she packs the side of a channel with a bar and a bucket of rubble from first light to the sixth hour, in silence, and is not asked anything, and Adrian does not ask her anything and is not going to.** **AND SHE IS NOT THE KEEPER OF THE REDROOT GATE, and the two must never share a verb, a tool, a material, a room or a light again — see the ruling below.**

**A woman of about twenty-four.** In seven chapters (0251, 0252, 0253, 0254, 0255, 0256, 0257, 0258, 0259) and **her second handle is a slate she turns over and writes the date on the back of**, and about four people in each room see her do it and none of them says a word. She reads a document out, copies four places under her own hand, wrote *safe* and *villages* on two lines of a wall in a room over a yard and did not join them up, read the date back twice from a plain slate in Chapter 0256, and **said a figure of ninety-four gates out loud in a market and said in the same breath that nobody in four hundred miles has ever counted them. It is not a count and no later writer may treat it as one.**

**The woman of about fifty-six who keeps a shop four streets down from a market.** In one chapter (0255). Nine measures of salt under her counter since the thirty-ninth day of the Long Month, gone hard, and she moved all nine up onto the counter in two goes and down into the back room in one, with her arms and not on a board, **broke none and opened none and did not put a thumb into one to see whether the middle had gone soft**, and by the tenth hour could not close her right hand all the way round a trowel. She wrote a hundred pages of dates in a day-book and then wrote nothing at the head of the hundredth day of the Long Month, **because there is no hundred and first day of anything in this world**, and carried the book to a market and said three words: there is no form. **Break is not open and no chapter may say what a wage in salt is worth.**

**The man of about thirty-nine who trades on the Open Hand board.** In six chapters (0252, 0254, 0255, 0257, 0258, 0259) and **he is a man in every one of them and the repair pass fixed the one place where a chapter had made him a woman.** He writes figures on the back of his own hand and puts his hand flat over them, does not offer to carry a thing he could carry, gives a reason about a queue and is not thanked for it, went to the door in the middle of Chapter 0257 and came back and said nothing, **carried two slates between two rooms in Chapter 0252 and is the carrier in every instance of it in this batch, and in Chapter 0258 copied the nine lines of a wall onto a slate with a cloth before the room opened because a wall of chalk nine miles up a road is not a thing anybody can carry into a room.**

**The man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall and wrote the ninth line.** In three chapters (0253, 0254, 0255) and he wrote the store on a page on purpose in the ninth line of the posting on the forty-sixth day of the Long Month and has not moved it and **has now refused five times to say whether the line works and keeps five refusals and may not be asked a sixth in a yard.** **He is a fifth person and not a woman and is never in a sentence with any of the four women of about thirty-four.**

**THE FOUR WOMEN OF ABOUT THIRTY-FOUR ARE FOUR AND A FIFTH IS NOT A PHANTOM.** **The one in the store on the upper road, who is on no list in it** (0253, 0258: a pail in each hand, comes about nine feet short of the sack and does not come nearer, and will not put a boot on the nine feet). **The one who keeps a stall by a wharf** (0252, 0260: puts dates on doors, is the person in this city who puts them on; in 0260 she walks the whole way up the road past this city before he does, has a piece of paper in the front of her coat with nothing on the top of it and a slate face down on a chest she has not turned up, **writes the question in her own hand and does not sign it, and the reason is hers and it is nine years old, and her name is on a page in the world and on no page you can read**). **The one from a fourth household on the road** (0259: silent all day in a room, writes the asking on a slate without speaking, and is the one who carried a broom up the bank of the cut on the ninety-second day of the Long Month and put it down about nine feet from the keeper of the Redroot gate and said nothing about what it was for). **The one in the house at the bottom of the cut** (not in these ten chapters). **The fourth-household woman and the stall-by-a-wharf woman are two people and Chapters 0259 and 0260 do not merge them and now four files say so in writing.**

**THE KEEPER OF THE REDROOT GATE — 38, and NOT IN ANY OF THESE TEN CHAPTERS, and the reason that took a review to establish.** She is at zero across the ten files: not named, not counted, not on a bank, not in a yard, not referred to, and the string *Redroot* at zero. **THAT WAS NEVER THE TEST, because her presence in this manuscript is not a word: it is a woman who works the invert of a channel that has had no water in it since the forty-first day of the Long Month, with a stick, from the first hour to the eleventh, in silence, with brooms on boards at the top of the cut and the light going off the far side of it. Chapter 0260 as first written gave Alys Kern all of it, and a reader would have read her as the keeper.** She is now physically apart from Alys Kern in verb, tool, material, room and light. **A later writer may not give the Redroot keeper's working, her tool, her silence-among-others, her boards or her light to anybody, and the check is a person-by-person read and not a search.** She is eighty-one days into a year of being angry at day 260, which is derived and on no page and in no mouth; the year is not over.

**THE MAN OF ABOUT THIRTY-ONE WITH THE HAND THAT WILL NOT CLOSE.** In one mention (0254), in a list of two people who could not be given the question in writing four days beforehand, and **he is now *a man of about thirty-one at a stall by the wharf, with a hand that will not close*, which is `bible/characters.md`'s own descriptor for him and attributes nothing to him. A hand that opened once on its own on the eighty-first day of the Long Month is still not a thing anybody in this city has a word for, no figure for it is on a page in Chapters 0251 to 0260, and this batch made it neither a symptom nor a cure.**

**The woman of about thirty-nine who cannot get a rack above her shoulder.** In one mention (0254), in the same list, and she has not shown anybody her refusal and may not be asked about it in a yard.

**The girl of seventeen.** In two chapters (0254, 0256). On the front of no page and cannot be fetched and being unfindable is not a qualification and this volume does not resolve her. **She has a plain slate that is not her own, because her own has her name at the top of it, and that is the only reason there are two slates in this city and not one.** She copied five figures in her own hand and put no mark beside any of them, and she read the date, the name and the reason back twice in the same words, the second time more slowly, and then wrote a date at the head of a page in a form that had not existed when she was born.

**The woman of about seventy of a village of nine households that has refused four times.** In one chapter (0256), and she said no before the notary was in the room, and her reason is hers and is correct and is not good: a name given to a month is a name a clerk will use, and nobody in that building will be able to tell whether it means the month she means. She said at the end that she had been a person somebody was asked for before and that it is not a comfortable thing to be used for, and **that a refusal nobody records is not a refusal.**

**The man of about forty who carries sacks.** In one chapter (0251), paid by the load and not by the errand, a hand on the lid for three hours that did not move once, and he said out loud that the only trade he has is the difference between a thing shut for a night in front of nine people and a thing shut for a night in front of nobody.

**And nobody else is new.** No new person was introduced in these ten chapters beyond Alys Kern and the woman of about fifty-six, who is on a page from Volume 04, and Sera Vail, who is on a page from Volume 02. **Marrow is in no chapter of this batch, the man of about fifty-two who digs is in no chapter of this batch, the man of about thirty-two on the bank is in no chapter of this batch and is in no sentence with Adrian Vale, and the man of about twenty-seven who washes the board is in one and has still not written on it and has still not washed the wall above it.**

---

## Volume 06, Batch 0002 — Chapters 0261 to 0270, days 261 to 270. APPEND-ONLY. The block above is Volume 06 Batch 0001's and nothing in it is superseded. Where this file and a chapter disagree about a day, a name or a figure, the chapter is right. Batch record: `state/batch-summaries/volume-06-batch-0002.md`, and §14 of it is the review repair pass, and this block has the repair's rulings in it.

**THE MAP OF THE PEOPLE, AND IT IS THE REASON THIS BLOCK EXISTS.** Twelve descriptors and no names are on a page in these ten chapters, and a name arrives in this book in a room in daylight with a date on it by a person, and nobody in these ten chapters put a name to a page. **Every person below is a descriptor and is a person, and the twelve are these and not more:** a smith of about sixty with a yard on the third street off this market (0261); a boy of about thirteen, his apprentice, who goes out on a Tuesday, a Wednesday and a Friday with a hinge, a length of chain and a bar of tallow and a knotted string on the cart rail (0261, 0268); a man of about fifty-five at the first gate of the road, with a bar of oak shod with iron and a chain over a pulley (0261, 0263); **a man of about thirty-six of a market town who keeps nothing, holds gates on a turn, and is the man who said the name of the gate out loud in a room in daylight (0264 to 0270)**; a woman of about thirty at a stall in that town who organises pail lines, mends nets, carries a pot on her head, got nine houses to run a line nobody ordered, and took back what she had said about it (0264, 0265, 0266, 0267, 0269, 0270); a man of about twenty-six of that town who asked to stop being a guest in nine words (0267, 0268); a woman of about thirty-three at a bread board in four hundred miles (0268); a woman of about thirty-three of the second village who said the word at a door and did not know where she had had it (0269); a man of about forty-two with two mules and nine sacks (0269); a man of about forty-three of the fourth household at the low end of the channel, whose left arm is out for nine days (0265, 0266); a woman of about forty-six of that household, with a sieve (0265, 0266, 0270); a boy of about eleven with a yoke and two pails (0266); a man of about thirty-five, a carter, with a load going nine days up the road and a tarpaulin lashed at four corners (0268).

**AND THE RULING, WHICH THE REPAIR PASS HAD TO APPLY A SECOND TIME IN THIS BATCH.** The four women of about thirty-four are four, and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall is a fifth person, and the ruling at `state/batch-summaries/volume-06-batch-0001.md` §14.7 is binding and is restated in four files. **The writer's own pass found that Chapter 0267's man of about thirty-four who asked to stop being a guest was not the man who keeps a stall, and that Chapter 0268 then called the same person a *woman* of about thirty-four, which would have put a fifth woman of about thirty-four on a page and put her in one breath with the woman of the stall by a wharf. He is a man of about twenty-six in both chapters, and that is fixed. Chapter 0268's bread-board woman is a woman of about thirty-three and not thirty-one, so that she cannot be confused with the man of about thirty-one at a stall by a wharf with a hand that will not close, and the woman at the door in the second village in 0269 is a *third* woman of about thirty-three and is also not that man. Every remaining *about thirty-four* in the ten files is the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall, and he is in Chapter 0262 and at the end of 0263 and in no other. This is the second time this repository has found a gender or age slip in a chapter about a roll, the first being the Open Hand trader in Chapter 0254, and it is why the check is a reading and not a string.**

**Adrian Vale — 32, protagonist. Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing, and NOTHING IN THIS BATCH MOVED THAT.** On two of the ten pages (0269, 0270) and performed no working, opened no threshold, was offered no workway and asked for none, spent no passage privilege, and **was not asked a question about a gate in any of the ten chapters.** In 0269 he walks the road west with about eleven people and explains the first way twice at two gates and says the word *safe* once and will not say it again. **In 0270 he asks Tamsin Quill about the second way on the fifth sheet without saying what the working would cost her, she catches it in one sentence, and he is the object of the fracture and not its author, and that is the whole of what is wrong with it and it is unpaid. Chapter 0272 pays it and Chapter 0271 may not.** His gains are zero and the things on the page are not powers: four knots, a bar, a dry channel, a pail line nobody ordered, a rack that does not come off, nine names on a wall, a sheet read out in four kitchens by accident, a word attached to nobody, a woman who found out what a page costs, and a woman who found out what a second way costs. **None of them is a power. One of them is the price of a power. All of them are heavier than a power.**

**Tamsin Quill.** In two chapters (0269, 0270) and on 0269 she says out loud that a man in a good coat is the only person in four hundred miles who cannot be checked by anybody in the room he is standing in, and that it has cost the road about a week of being talked about, and that he has about nine days of three villages in his coat and will not open the sheet on a gatepost. **In 0270 she brings a book of Crown Law nine hundred miles in a bag at the bottom of a kitchen and does not open it, says she has not read the second way out in three villages in four days and will not read it at a kitchen table with the man who asked for it, and then says the whole of the fracture in about four sentences and does not apologise and does not make it smaller by saying it nicely.** *Open, and paid in Chapter 0272, and the two of them are not reconciled in Chapter 0270 and may not be.*

**The man of forty-one in a very good coat.** In four chapters (0264, 0268, 0269, 0270) and he is a channel and not a favour. **His second handle is the inside of a coat, and in 0264 he opens no one of the five sheets and thanks nobody and is gone down the road by the seventh hour with four of the eleven walking him as far as the first gate and no further.** In 0269 he says the word *safe* once at a gatepost and will not say it again, and stands about nine paces off a chain so as not to be counted as being at the gate, and comes back down the road at the eighth hour with his coat buttoned and no sheet open and about nine people in that market are going to ask him on Monday what the first way is. In 0270 he is the reason a man of about thirty-six carried a bar two hundred paces to a gate he could not bar.

**The man of about fifty-five at the first gate of the road.** In two chapters (0261, 0263) and **he is the only person in four hundred miles anybody could put in the third place of the Crown's schedule.** He keeps it because he opens it, he put a chain on it because a cart came through in the night in the first month, he has never been asked anything at all in a hundred days, and he asked for one thing and it was *do not come back with it in a book*. **He is not named, he is not in a column, and this city's refusal to name him is not free, because a refusal leaves a page nine hundred miles away free to send somebody who will. He may not be asked again in a chapter before Chapter 0276 and not in a clerk's mouth.**

**The man of about thirty-six of the market town.** In six chapters (0264, 0265, 0269, 0270) and he is the volume's argument in a person. **He keeps nothing, he holds gates on a turn, and he is the only one in the ten who said the name of a gate out loud in a room in daylight with a date on the door, and he is not on the roll that went up on the wall, and that is correct and not a fault.** He gave the batch's first complete answer to anything — a post, about nine days of hands, a bed dug by hand, a rack, four days of water carried — and said that a person who cannot be given a cost cannot be made to answer for it. **On day 270 he carried a bar to a gate he could not bar because a man in a very good coat stood nine paces off it and asked him to, and he has never done a thing to that gate for a stranger in nine years, and he said he had not slept about it. His second handle is the spade, which he does not put down.**

**The woman of about thirty at the stall.** In six chapters (0264, 0265, 0266, 0267, 0269, 0270) and she is the batch's best new person and **the only person in the ten who changed her mind about something she had claimed.** On the tenth day of the Short Month she got nine houses to send down a pail line nobody ordered and said out loud that she made the line herself. On the eleventh she went to a kitchen and took it back, naming the day and the hour she said it and giving the reason, which is that she said it to a stranger and it was not true, and said the line ran itself and was not organised and was not written down and was not hers. She organises pail lines, mends a net through a company's first hour without looking up, says there is no word for the eight guests and there is not going to be one made in that room, and tells a man in a good coat at a gatepost that the cost of writing down a turn is nine people who have never been on a page. **Her second handle is a pot she carries on her head, and she is not going to be thanked for any of it and has said so twice.**

**The woman of about forty-six of the fourth household, with a sieve.** In four chapters (0265, 0266, 0270) and **she is the household the volume's finding of day 294 is about.** She went into a channel in clay with both hands on her husband's collar and asked nobody for anything and nobody in the hole asked her for anything. She said in her own kitchen that a household's days are not a schedule and refused to say what her days are, and the reason is hers and it is nine years old and it is not the sentence from Chapter 0154 and is not that sentence said differently. She works the clay out of about nine buckets of water with a sieve for six hours of a Monday and does not look up while a fracture is said in her kitchen, and she is the one who works out that a road has been kept for about a hundred and forty years by a bent bar, a post out in the water and a person who is not the last one to have held it. **Her second handle is the sieve, and it is down on the lip of the channel for about a minute and a half every time she has finished saying a thing that matters.**

**The man of about forty-three of the fourth household.** In two chapters (0265, 0266), nine days of no use with the left arm, counted in the hole, and **he does not want the use of it back for nine days and the nine days is the same nine days the man from the town quoted on Saturday about a post, and the two of them have never spoken and nobody has put them together.** He feeds the fire with his right hand at the fifth hour because he has got up at the fifth hour for about nine years. *Open, and the arm is a private figure and is not an interval chain and may not be measured off an anchor.*

**The register-keeper — the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register.** In one chapter (0262) and **she is the only decision any person makes in these ten chapters.** She named the cost before the thing, three times, and then announced she was going to put a number on her second slate and did not, and the reason is that a list of who was not asked is a list with a name in it, which is a Hearthguard of forty-four's sentence from the fourth day of the Short Month. She then wrote one line with the day, the question and the room's failure to answer, **no name and no figure**, and said what she bought with it. **On day 262 she reversed a decision of her own from day 257 and said in a mouth that she had decided it five days earlier. Her second handle is the second slate, and she now has a chalk in her hand for a second and does not use it.**

**And nobody else is new, and nobody got stronger, and nobody left.** The keeper of the Redroot gate is at zero across these ten files by name, by year, by count and by day, and the proof is a person-by-person read of every woman's physical signature and not a search, and it is at `state/batch-summaries/volume-06-batch-0002.md` §6.2 and §14. **Hettis Roke is in none of these ten chapters; his refusal of day 259 is referred to twice, correctly dated, and is not restaged.** Jonah Pike, Sera Vail, the notary of fifty, the two twenty-nine-year-old women, the man of about thirty-nine on the Open Hand board and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall are in none of these ten chapters except the last, who is in 0262 and says nothing for five hours. **The man of about thirty-two who said he was finished and not stopped is in none of these ten chapters and is in no sentence with Adrian Vale. The about four hundred and forty are not in these ten chapters and their chapter is Chapter 0279. The delegation has not left. Marrow is in none of these ten chapters. The girl of seventeen is in none of these ten chapters.**

## Volume 06, Batch 0003 — Chapters 0271 to 0280, days 271 to 280. APPEND-ONLY. The block above is Volume 06 Batch 0002's and nothing in it is superseded. Where this file and a chapter disagree about a day, a name or a figure, the chapter is right. Batch record: `state/batch-summaries/volume-06-batch-0003.md`, and §14 of it is the review repair pass, and this block has the repair's rulings in it.

**THE MAP OF THE PEOPLE, AND IT IS THE REASON THIS BLOCK EXISTS.** **Two people are new to a page in these ten chapters and both are descriptors and not names, and a name arrives in this book in a room in daylight with a date on it by a person:** **a man of about forty-nine who keeps a store at the north end of a market nine hundred paces past the last named house, has kept it about eleven years, pays about nine people out of a pocket out of a wooden bowl, and did not open on a Friday, and he is the reason a favour is recognisable as one** (0276, 0277); and **a man of about thirty-eight who keeps a tally on eleven miles of flats, has put a number in a return four times a year since the winter before the water, has never been answered and never had anybody come, and carries a flat of wood with notches cut in one edge and about nine of the notches worn smooth** (0279). **Also new to a page: a man of about thirty-seven of the outer ward, a Hearthguard, who has been nineteen years in a body of four men, came up the road with Roke, and puts a slate face down on a bench and does not open it** (0275, 0280); a carter's boy of about fifteen who puts his shoulder to a barrow for about a minute and goes on his way (0278). **Carried from earlier batches and on a page in these ten: the man of about forty-three of the fourth household and the woman of about forty-six with a sieve (0271, 0278); the boy of about eleven with a yoke (0271); Tamsin Quill (0272); Adrian Vale (0272, 0274, 0275); the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall (0272, 0273); the woman of about twenty-four (0272, 0275, 0280); the man of about forty-one in a very good coat (0273, 0279); the man of about thirty-six of the market town (0273 to 0280); the woman of about thirty at the stall (0271, 0276, 0277, 0278); Hettis Roke (0275, 0280); the woman of about thirty-three at a bread board (0276, 0279); the man of about twenty-six who asked to stop being a guest (0277); the woman of about thirty-four by a wharf (0279); the girl of seventeen (0279).**

**AND THE FOUR RULINGS THAT HAD TO BE APPLIED, AND WHICH OF THEM CAUGHT SOMETHING.** **The four women of about thirty-four are four and the man of about thirty-four who keeps a stall is a fifth person.** The man of about thirty-four is in Chapters 0272 and 0273 and **there is no woman of about thirty-four in either of them**; the woman of about thirty-four by the wharf is in Chapter 0279 and **there is no man of about thirty-four in it and no second woman of about thirty-four in it**, and the counter of the about four hundred and forty is a man of about thirty-eight and the girl is seventeen. **The two twenty-nine-year-old women:** Tamsin Quill is in Chapter 0272 in the Marches and the woman of about twenty-nine who keeps a public register is in Chapter 0273 in this city, and **they are never in one room, never in one sentence as one person, and nothing in these ten chapters settles whether they are one woman.** The keeper of the Redroot gate is at zero in every form including her working, and **that check is a person-by-person read and not a search, at the record's §6.2.1.** And **the man of about thirty-six of the market town is a man and the gatekeeper of that market is his to keep and is not hers to become.**

**Adrian Vale — 32, protagonist. Stage 2, Passage holder, in the doing, and NOTHING IN THIS BATCH MOVED THAT.** On three of the ten pages and named in two of them — named in 0272 and 0275, and present and unnamed in 0274 — performed no working, opened no threshold, was offered no workway and asked for none, spent no passage privilege, **was not asked a question about a gate in any of the ten chapters, was not fetched by anybody and fetched himself.** The only things he does in these ten days are withdraw an asking, find out that he cannot hand a question to a body, and work out alone in a yard that the plan he built was the same act done faster by a man who had never done it faster before. **The chapter says he worked it out and does not say he was right, and nobody in that room told him and nobody in the yard offered.** His gains are zero and the things on the page are not powers: a sieve in a left hand, a withdrawn asking, a fifth condition nobody has, a word with two syllables in it, a body that cannot be asked, four sentences and a fifth, a bowl of coins, eight absences, a barrow on its side, a number said out loud on boards, and a keeper instead of a holder. **None of them is a power. One of them is the price of a power. All of them are heavier than a power.**

**Tamsin Quill.** In one chapter (0272) and **she is the only person in this batch who names a price and refuses to make it smaller.** A name on a register is a name in a book, a book is kept, a name in a book can be looked up, and there is no office between that table and the place the days are supposed to go. **He withdrew the asking at the sixth hour before he set out, and she then said there is a fifth condition, that there always was, that it is about the man asking it and not about the question, and that she is not going to hand it to him. Nobody said it was a misunderstanding, nobody forgave anybody, and a thing that has been paid is not the same as a thing that has been mended.** *Open. A later batch may not have her forgive it, may not have the fifth condition given, and may not print the question itself.*

**Hettis Roke, forty-four, of the Hearthguard of the outer ward, nineteen years in.** In two chapters (0275, 0280) and **in the midpoint he refuses the first way on the Hearthguard's behalf in four sentences and a fifth, the refusal binds a company, and it is dated to the instrument and not to the day.** He said all five at the same speed and did not stop at the end of them. *Open, and `outline/volume-06.md` §12 also dates to this volume the asking of this man about his own eight, and nobody asked him in these ten chapters.*

**The man of about forty-three of the fourth household.** In two chapters (0271, 0278) and **his left arm went into the clay on the tenth day of the Short Month, a Sunday, and not on the ninth, which two earlier state files give** — the page is Chapter 0265 and a later writer must re-derive it from there. It came back on the sixteenth. **The chapter's lead said it came back on the sixth day of a nine, and the six was a real count belonging to a different interval: six days out of the clay, with the ninth day of the Short Month being the day the nine was said on and the sixteenth being the seventh day of it. The lead, the chapter's title and a state block all carried the merged ordinal and all three are corrected.** He can put a thing on a shelf with that hand and cannot lift about four buckets, took the sieve out of his wife's hands for a minute and a half, will not be thanked, will not be given the pot, and is at the low end of a channel with a sieve and about four buckets a day instead of nine. *Open, and the arm is a private figure and is not an interval chain, and it has two anchors and both of them are named in the chapter.*
